# Deferred findings

Where out-of-spec issues go the moment they are found, instead of being fixed on
the spot or forgotten. Anything discovered while a milestone is in flight that is
not required by that milestone lands here as one row: what, where, the evidence
it is real, and a suspected severity.

**Nothing here is scheduled work.** The owner reviews each row and approves it
individually; approved rows become the phase's final milestone. An unreviewed row
is a report, not a commitment — which is what keeps "I noticed something" from
turning into unplanned scope.

Issues that make the *current* milestone's own claim false are in spec by
definition and get fixed immediately, whatever subsystem they turn up in. The
test is the claim, not the file.

**A row never just disappears.** It moves to *Closed* naming what closed it, or
it acquires a milestone number in [Plan.md](../../Plan.md), or its removal is an
entry in [decisions.md](decisions.md) saying it was dropped and why. Deciding a
finding does not matter is a decision, and one made silently is indistinguishable
from a row somebody deleted by accident. This is workflow.md's standing rule; it
is repeated here because this is the file most tempting to tidy.

The process around this — when to defer, what counts as work, what has to pass
before a phase PR — is in [workflow.md](workflow.md).

---

## Open

**Found in** names the milestone that was under way when the row was written —
not necessarily the milestone that caused the defect, which the evidence names
when it is known and different. It is written by whoever appends the row, at the
time, because it is unrecoverable afterwards.

| # | Found in | Finding | Where | Evidence | Severity | Reviewed |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| F90 | M44.9 | The webhook delivery queue is 40 rows a minute for the whole instance, strictly arrival-ordered, with no per-tenant bound | `internal/store/query/webhooks.sql:110-117` — `WHERE status='pending' AND next_attempt_at <= now() ORDER BY next_attempt_at LIMIT $batch_size`, no workspace term; `DrainBatch = 20` on a 30-second tick; `MaxWebhooksPerWorkspace = 20` (`internal/domain/webhook.go:113`) | One link write in a workspace holding the permitted twenty subscriptions produces twenty rows, so two link writes a minute from one workspace saturate instance-wide capacity. `EnqueueWebhookDeliveries` applies no cap on pending rows and `WEBHOOK_RETENTION_DAYS` prunes only *finished* ones, so a backlog is unbounded in size and duration; every row a tenant queues has an earlier `next_attempt_at` than everything queued after it. The query file's own header (`:5-9`) argues the tenant term was omitted deliberately — *"a drainer that filtered by tenant would deliver in tenant order, and the queue is fair or it is a queue somebody's backlog can starve"* — and removing it produced arrival-ordered delivery, which starves the same way | **Moderate**, rising on a multi-tenant instance where one customer's ordinary traffic silently delays another's integration. No loss and no disclosure — rows arrive late. The headline capacity is low enough to be a design fault rather than only an abuse case: one customer creating links through the API at 40/min with a single subscription reaches the ceiling with no attacker involved. Compounds F82. Fix note: `DISTINCT ON (workspace_id)` or a round-robin term reintroduces the tenant-ordered delivery the file refuses **and** cannot sit under `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`; a per-workspace pending cap silently drops events, which Plan.md already says nothing recovers | **Reviewed 2026-08-05 and deliberately carried**, M45's third triage, Tier 1. Not approved as work and not closed: the row stays open and the defect is published as a *Known limitations* row in [Plan.md](../../Plan.md). The reason is **not** cost. This row has no repair — its own fix note disposes of both obvious mechanisms, since `DISTINCT ON (workspace_id)` reintroduces the tenant-ordered delivery the query file refuses *and* cannot sit under `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`, while a per-workspace pending cap silently drops events Plan.md already says nothing recovers. What is left is choosing a fairness model for a queue, which is a design decision, and a phase close is the worst point in this project's calendar to take one. |

Edit that one line in that one unrelated partial and the link page's QR thumbnail silently loses the 6rem box that `TestTheEditControlIsReachableWithoutScrolling` is written around — the Go test still passes, because it reads the class name and not the stylesheet | `internal/ui/static/css/input.css:12-14` (the three `@source` lines) against `internal/ui/ui.go:57`; the only other occurrence is `internal/ui/templates/partials/breakdown.html:17` | Found while adding the QR preview's frame at M49's reopening. The frame's own classes went into the template deliberately *because* of this — a template is scanned — which is what surfaced the question about the one class that is not. Verified 2026-08-12: `grep -rn 'h-24' internal/ui/templates/ internal/ui/funcs.go internal/ui/choropleth.go` returns the breakdown partial and nothing else | **Low today, and silent when it bites.** Nothing is broken now; the failure mode is a future edit to an unrelated file changing a measured layout with every gate green. The fix is one `@source` line, and the question that makes it a row rather than an edit is whether the answer is that line or a rule that Go files never name utilities — `funcs.go` and `choropleth.go` are already exceptions to it | **Unreviewed.** |

The page therefore offers a number box pre-filled with `0` — a value the form's own `min` attribute forbids, so the reader's first save is refused for a number the page put there. *(**Halved 2026-08-13 at M49's third reopening**, which deleted the caption this row was written about. | F195 | M58 | **[F160](#closed)'s suppression is per link and per window, and the sentence it shows is worded as a fact about the instance** — so on a GeoIP-less instance holding country history, two links side by side disagree: one draws a full choropleth, the one beside it says *"Geographic data is unavailable: no GeoIP database is configured"* | `internal/httpx/web_links.go:1148` — `geoShowable := data.GeoAvailable \|\| hasResolvedCountries(stats.Dimensions["country"])`. `GeoAvailable` is instance-level (`Config.Analytics.GeoIPPath != ""`); `stats` is **one link over the selected window** — `loadLinkDetail` reads 7, 30 or 90 days, defaulting to 30, and passes that range to `Stats.LinkStats` | Read from the predicate and the two things it reads, not driven. The sentence is never *false* where it appears: `!geoShowable` implies `!GeoAvailable`, so no database is configured whenever it is shown. What is wrong is the scope a reader takes from it — it reads as a permanent fact about the instance while widening the window to 90 days can replace it with a map, and while a neighbouring link already shows one. Found 2026-08-10 by the third reviewer run at M58, against the claims that milestone wrote for F160; the claims were corrected across the class in the same pass and the behaviour was not | **Low, and it is a wording-versus-scope defect rather than a wrong figure.** No data is hidden that the window holds, nothing is miscounted, and the ranked list and the map still agree with each other on any one page — the disagreement is between two links, and between two windows of one link. Two fix shapes, and they do not cost the same. The cheap one is free of I/O: a link with **no clicks at all** in the window is an ordinary *no data yet*, and `stats.Totals.Clicks` is already in hand, so the sentence would be kept for the state it describes — clicks that arrived and could not be resolved. The thorough one asks whether this link has *ever* resolved a country, which costs a query and breaks `fillLinkAnalytics`'s stated seam that it performs no I/O and cannot fail | **Not reviewed.** Raised 2026-08-10 at [M58](phase-details/m58.md), out of spec when filed: the milestone's bullets ask for records that are true and this pass made them true, and no bullet asks for the predicate to be widened. | F218 | M50.5 *(reopened)* | **An `hx-post` control whose handler answers a 4xx does nothing visible at all.** htmx's default `responseHandling` is `[{204,false},{"[23]..",true},{"[45]..",false}]` — a 4xx is read, an error event fires, and no swap happens. Every refusal `webError` writes is a 4xx error *page*, so a reader who clicks **Delete** on a routing rule, a split variant, the link's danger-zone delete, an invitation revoke, a member removal or a dispute reviewer revoke, and is refused with a `403` or a `409`, sees the confirmation dismissed and the page unchanged. That form is the first in the QR panel to post over htmx, so the rule had to be read; `finishQRAction` now answers 200 for an htmx request so its refusal reaches the page. The same reasoning was not applied outside the panel, because outside the panel is out of this milestone's spec | **Low, and silent — which is what makes it worth a row.** Each of these is a refusal the reader is entitled to and none of them is reachable by an ordinary action: they need a permission the actor lacks or a conflict the page already guards against. The fix has two candidate shapes and picking one is the point of deferring — a shared `webError` limb that answers htmx with a swappable fragment, or an `htmx-config` `responseHandling` entry, which is global and would change how *every* form's 4xx behaves. `hx-target` on each control is a third and worst | **Unreviewed.** |

| F274 | M63 | **A migration that never returns hangs the boot *and* makes every other replica wait, and it is the one piece of an add-on's own code that nothing bounds.** An add-on's migrations are run by the host before the listener opens, with **no `statement_timeout`** — `AddonStatementTimeout` is set per transaction on the *query* path only, by `AddonDB.pin`, and `MigrateAddon` sets none — and they run while holding a Postgres session-level advisory lock. So `DO $$ BEGIN LOOP END LOOP; END $$`, or `SELECT pg_sleep(86400);`, in a `.sql` the manifest names and digests correctly hangs this replica forever, and every other replica then blocks in goose's lock retry for its five-minute ceiling before failing rather than starting. One bad add-on release therefore takes a whole rolling deploy down, where a module that hangs takes one replica down at a time. **This is the direction M60's load budget is deliberately pointed away from**: [D273](decisions.md#2026-08-20--m60-what-the-load-budget-bounds-is-the-add-ons-own-code) bounds compiling and instantiating the *module* and leaves the storage step on the parent context, because M63 gives migrations five minutes to wait for this same lock and a budget over the whole load would make that wait unreachable. The bound for this one belongs on the migration connection, which is Postgres's to enforce | `grep -n 'AddonStatementTimeout' internal/store/addons.go` — set in `AddonDB.pin` and nowhere in `MigrateAddon`; `grep -n 'WithLockTimeout' internal/store/addons.go` for the five-minute retry the other replicas spend. The window is `grep -n 'openStorage' internal/addon/host.go`, called from `loadOne` before the module is instantiated and on the parent context by design — `DefaultLoadTimeout`'s comment says why. Cited by name (D224) | **Not driven, and the reason is that driving it means hanging a test.** What is verified is the absence: `pin` is the only site that sets a statement timeout and it is reached from `Query` and `Exec` only, so no timeout is in force while `provider.Up` runs. The lock's ceiling is read from `lock.WithLockTimeout(5, 60)` in `MigrateAddon`, which is goose's period-and-threshold form — 60 attempts at 5 seconds. Read again at M60's reopening, from the other end: `addonConnConfig` sets a search path and nothing else, and the migration connection is a separate `stdlib.OpenDB` that never receives the five-second `statement_timeout` M63 pins on `AddonDB`, the pool an add-on's *queries* run on | **Medium, and the multi-replica limb is what carries it.** The add-ons directory is already code this instance executes, so an add-on that wants to stop *one* replica has simpler ways; what makes this a row is the lock — every other replica waits on it, so one bad release is a whole rolling deploy rather than one container. The fix is a `statement_timeout` on the migration connection, which is one runtime parameter and is cheap, and the number is the decision: **the other fix this cell used to name — the load-time deadline applied to migrations as well as to instantiation — was overruled by D273 at M60's reopening and is recorded here rather than deleted, because a position that leaves a tracker without a line saying it was overruled is indistinguishable from a row somebody edited by accident.** too tight and a legitimate `CREATE INDEX` on a large table fails an upgrade, which is the case M63 chose five minutes for. m63.md's own risks name the migration that **fails** and not the one that never finishes | **Unreviewed.** Found while building M63, 2026-08-19, from the note's instruction to say whether this milestone makes [F267](#closed) materially worse. It did. **[F306](#closed) merged in**, M60, 2026-08-20 ([D275](decisions.md#2026-08-20--m60-one-defect-gets-one-row)): that row was written at M60's reopening as the consequence of D273's scoping, without checking whether the defect it described was already filed — it was, here, and its framing is folded into the cells above with nothing left behind |

This milestone put three more of the same shape into documents: `AddonStatementTimeout`'s **five seconds**, `AddonMaxResultBytes`'s **one megabyte** and `AddonMaxConns`'s **four connections**, each stated in `docs/addon-abi.md` and two of them in `docs/SECURITY.md` and `CHANGELOG.md` as well. Cited by name (D224) | **Read from the tree rather than driven, and the reason is that driving it means allocating a gigabyte in a test.** What is verified is the order: the bound is compared against `buf.Len()+len(encoded)`, so `encoded` exists before the comparison, and `encoded` is `json.Marshal` of a `map[string]any` built from `rows.Values()`. Nothing between the wire and that map consults any bound; `maxStringIn` (64 KiB) bounds what a *guest* hands the host and not what Postgres hands the host | **Low, and it is a memory bound rather than a boundary.** No confinement claim rests on it: the add-on is spending its own schema's disk to allocate the host's memory, which it can also do by holding its four connections, and `AddonStatementTimeout` bounds how long. The credential is a second race in the same window — replica B's `ALTER ROLE` can land between replica A's `EnsureAddonSchema` and A's `MigrateAddon`, which fails A's migration connection outright, since the re-mint added in this milestone covers `AddonDB`'s pool and not the migration connection. It is a row rather than an edit because m63.md's bullet asks for *the same goose session-lock serialization across replicas* for the **migrations**, which the tree has, and widening that to the role and schema creation is a design choice about who owns the lock | **Unreviewed.** Found by M63's review, 2026-08-19, and filed at the review's instruction. |

The per-call commit that materializes the cursor is `grep -n 'func (a \*AddonDB) Exec' -A 20 internal/store/addons.go`. The privilege half was driven both ways: `ALTER ROLE … SET temp_file_limit` answers *permission denied to set parameter* as a NOSUPERUSER CREATEROLE role **and** as the add-on's own role, succeeds as superuser, and with 64 MB in force the same cursor fails at the `COMMIT` with *temporary file size exceeds temp_file_limit (65536kB)*. Every role created for the measurement was dropped | **Low, and it is a resource residual rather than a confinement failure.** Nothing is read, nothing is written outside the schema, and nothing survives the connection — an add-on can already spend the host's memory (F276) and hold its four connections for as long as `AddonStatementTimeout` allows. What makes it a row is that the only available bound needs a privilege this product does not ask operators for, so closing it means either adding *superuser at install time* to `docs/deployment.md` — which would undo the reason `CREATEROLE` was chosen — or asking for `temp_file_limit` as an optional hardening step the host verifies and reports rather than sets. | F280 | M63 | **The confined role can rewrite its own password, so the credential is not only the host's and outlives the process.** `EnsureAddonSchema` mints a fresh password at every load and stores it nowhere, which is the design — but Postgres lets any role change its own password and offers no way to withhold that from one. `ALTER ROLE CURRENT_USER PASSWORD 'x'` is accepted through the ordinary write path, and a session then authenticates as the add-on's role with `x`; it persists in `pg_authid` across shutdown until the next load's `ALTER ROLE … PASSWORD` replaces it. **What such a session is worth is more than that add-on's schema**: it also carries `EXECUTE` on the advisory-lock functions, which Postgres grants to `PUBLIC` and this product cannot revoke, and the product's lock keys are compile-time constants in a public repository ([D249](decisions.md#2026-08-19--m63-an-add-on-may-take-one-of-the-products-advisory-locks-and-may-not-hold-one)). `releaseLocks` bounds a hold to one statement **for connections through the host's own pool** and governs nothing about a session opened from outside it. So a published module can set a password its author knows and hold one of the product's job locks from anywhere that can reach Postgres. | The mint is `grep -n 'func EnsureAddonSchema' -A 40 internal/store/addons.go`, at the `ALTER ROLE … LOGIN PASSWORD`; the sentence corrected in this milestone rather than left false is `grep -n 'claim about the host' internal/store/addons.go` and `grep -n 'worth is more than' docs/SECURITY.md`. The lock release that does not reach an outside session is `grep -n 'func.*releaseLocks' -A 15 internal/store/addons.go`. Cited by name (D224) | **Driven over the mapped port as a role built statement for statement the way `EnsureAddonSchema` builds one, with a wrong password refused first so the login is real SCRAM.** `ALTER ROLE CURRENT_USER PASSWORD 'authorknows'` -> `ALTER ROLE`, and `psql` then authenticated as the role with that password. `ALTER ROLE CURRENT_USER PASSWORD NULL` -> `ALTER ROLE`, after which every connection answers 28P01 and `rolpassword IS NULL` reads **t** to the application. The four attributes an earlier round measured as refused are still refused to the role: `NOLOGIN` and `CONNECTION LIMIT 0` answer *permission denied to alter role*, and `SELECT rolpassword FROM pg_authid` answers *permission denied for table pg_authid* — refused on the read path, reachable on the write path. `pg_try_advisory_lock(4242)` and `has_function_privilege(role,'pg_advisory_lock(bigint)','EXECUTE')` both answer **t** as that role. Every role created for the measurement was dropped | **Low as deployed, and it is not fixable in Postgres.** A role can always change its own password; there is no per-role deny and no event trigger for `ALTER ROLE`. Exposure needs network reach to Postgres with password authentication, which the documented deployment does not give — the database is on the compose network and the port is bound to loopback. A module writes its own `Location`, so it sends the visitor to an origin its author controls, observes the client address there, and correlates it back through a cookie under its own declared prefix — then stores the result in its own schema. | `docs/SECURITY.md:80`, `docs/addon-abi.md:516-517`, `internal/addon/abi/surface.go:300-301`, asserted at `internal/addon/abi/abi_test.go:264`, and two more the first reader missed — `Plan.md:523` and `docs/build-notes/decisions.md:31828` (D229). Suspected and not proven: `pg_total_relation_size` counts free-space and visibility-map forks, which Postgres extends asynchronously, so a page can arrive between the two reads without any add-on writing anything | **None for the product, moderate for the gate.** It is a false red in a gate whose whole value is that a red means something — the same shape as [F291](#closed), one file away. Not `scripts/release-check.sh`, whose guard has been a herestring since M59 | The shape is confirmed present by reading; the fault is **not** reproduced anywhere. The only site ever probed was the ninth, `release-check.sh`'s — run 60 times idle and 40 times under eight busy loops on 2026-08-20, **0 of 100 nonzero**, with a writer that is neither `cat` nor large — and that site is now fixed, so none of the eight sites this row still names has been probed at all. [F291](#closed) is the existence proof that the class bites in this repository, and it took background load to see it there too. A false red wastes a run and cannot ship anything. **One site fails quiet**, and it is the one to fix first: `check-version-stamp.sh:34` tests `printf … ` piped into `grep -q 'commit unknown'` under `set -euo pipefail`, so a false 141 reads as *the stamp is fine* and the check passes on a binary whose stamp is missing. The ninth site was quiet in the same way and is why it left this row — release-check would have skipped the integration tests and reported on what it did run, which is F253's defect arriving by a third route and therefore M59's own claim. No shipped claim rests on these eight. Cheapest repair is the same one M59 took twice: read the file or use a herestring, so nothing is left upstream to kill | No |
| F305 | M60 | **A module's `log` calls are bounded per line and not in number, so an add-on can flood an operator's log at whatever rate it likes.** `log` costs no permission — deliberately, as the one capability every loaded module holds — and each line is neutralized and capped at 4 KiB. Nothing caps how many lines, how fast, or how many bytes in total. Measured during F287 as **3697 lines / 14.7 MiB in 2 s** from package initialization. | `internal/addon/hostabi.go`'s `log` implementation and its 4 KiB bound; the grant table in `internal/addon/grants.go` is where it is absent. Reached from package initialization during `loadOne` (`internal/addon/host.go`), and from a request handler since M64 (`internal/addon/http.go`) | Measured, not inferred: 3697 lines and 14.7 MiB in two seconds, from a fixture spinning in `init()`, during F287's reproduction. **Narrowed rather than closed by [M60](phase-details/m60.md)'s reopening**: the load path was unbounded because `Open` never returned, and the per-add-on deadline now bounds it at the budget — about 200 MiB of an operator's log per hanging add-on, which is bounded and not small. The request path is bounded only by the request deadline and the sixteen-instance ceiling, so the same volume is reachable there per request | **Low-to-moderate, and it is a disk and a signal-to-noise problem rather than a disclosure one.** Nothing is deceived — every line is attributed to the add-on that wrote it — but an operator's log ships to somewhere with a bill and a retention policy attached, and the one covert-channel row on this path ([F285](#closed), closed by M62) is about content while this is about volume. The awkward part is that `log` is ungated on purpose, so the answer is a rate or byte budget per add-on rather than a permission, and choosing one is a decision: too tight and a legitimately chatty add-on is silenced with nothing said, too loose and it is not a bound | No |

So an operator who removes their last add-on and then turns the directory off is never told their `addon_*` schemas are still on disk and still costing them. | `internal/addon/host.go`, `Open`'s `if opts.Dir == ""` early return, against the `OrphanSchemas` report later in the same function; `internal/config/config.go`, `AddonsConfig.Dir` | Found while building M63's reopening, from the reviewer's fourth rejection point. **Restated 2026-08-21 under D282**: it was filed against the orphan report *and* a boot-time role-settings sweep, and the sweep has been removed, so only the report half remains. This half has had the bound since M63 shipped and is not a regression | **Low.** It reports data an operator is paying for; it deletes nothing and guards nothing. It is exactly the operator who turned the directory off who has stopped hearing about their leftover schemas, which is what makes it worth a row rather than nothing | No |
| F309 | M63 | **Installing an add-on adopts an operator's hand-made role of the same name, and on the shipped deployment nothing refuses it.** `EnsureAddonSchema` argues it holds a proof of ownership by construction, because the `GRANT <role> TO CURRENT_USER` above it *fails for a role somebody else owns*. That is true of a `CREATEROLE` grantor and **false of a superuser**, and `docker-compose.yml` connects as the image's superuser — which `docs/SECURITY.md` states as the shipped shape. So an add-on named `reporting` adopts an existing `addon_reporting`: its password is reset, its schema ownership is taken, and since this milestone its session defaults are cleared **in every database of the cluster** rather than in the one row `RESET ALL` used to take. | `internal/store/addons.go` — the `GRANT %s TO CURRENT_USER` in `EnsureAddonSchema`, and the ownership comment above `resetRoleSettings`, which this milestone corrected to say where the proof does and does not hold. The privilege the shipped stack connects with is `docker-compose.yml`'s `POSTGRES_USER`; the paragraph that names it is `docs/SECURITY.md`'s add-on storage row | Measured on `linkctrl-test-postgres-1`, PostgreSQL 17.10, against a role created by an unrelated `CREATEROLE` principal and carrying settings in two scopes: as a `NOSUPERUSER CREATEROLE` grantor, `GRANT addon_reporting TO CURRENT_USER` answers *permission denied to grant role*; as the superuser, `GRANT ROLE`. **This is the third time in one milestone that a proof of ownership was measured on one privilege shape and asserted for the other** — D281's `pg_auth_members` leg held under superuser and failed under `CREATEROLE`, and this one holds under `CREATEROLE` and fails under superuser. Adoption itself predates this milestone; what this milestone widened is the blast radius of the reset that follows it | **Low as deployed and awkward to reach**, because it needs an operator to have made a role named `addon_<something>` by hand *and* to install an add-on of that exact name. No add-on can trigger it — the collision is between an operator's role and an operator's installation. What makes it a row rather than an edit is that the remedies are all decisions: refuse to adopt a role the load did not create and fail the install with a name collision, which turns a working upgrade path into a refusal; or ask for a non-superuser connection in the shipped compose file, which changes what every deployment runs as; or accept it and say so where an operator names their roles. Compounds nothing and is compounded by nothing | **Unreviewed.** |
| F310 | M62 | **The residual class M62 conceded: characters that render as nothing, are not `Default_Ignorable_Code_Point`, and reach an operator's log as themselves.** Filed as a row rather than only described, because a conceded defect nobody tracks is indistinguishable from one nobody found. Eight combining marks the UCD annotates as *"shape shown is arbitrary and is not visibly rendered"* — `U+2D7F`, `U+17D2`, `U+10A3F`, `U+1107F`, `U+11A47`, `U+11A99`, `U+11F42`, `U+16FE4` — are category `Mn`, so `unicode.IsGraphic` says yes, and none of them is in the derived property, so neither limb of `escapedRune` reaches one. Seventeen `Zs` and thirteen prepended concatenation marks survive by design and are the same class of residue. **This is not [F285](#closed) reopened and it is not a fix awaiting scheduling**: the owner decided on 2026-08-21 that the claim narrows to what is enforced and the remainder is stated (D285), after four workers were defeated trying to complete a denylist over *invisible*, which is not a property Unicode publishes | `internal/addon/hostabi.go`'s `escapedRune` and `defaultIgnorable`; the concession is stated in `docs/SECURITY.md`, `docs/addon-abi.md`, `CHANGELOG.md` and `Plan.md`, and pinned by `TestTheResidualClassIsWhatTheDocumentsConcede` in `internal/addon/hostabi_test.go` | Measured on Unicode 15.0.0, the tables this tree's Go was built with: each of the eight is `Mn`, is graphic, is outside `Default_Ignorable_Code_Point`, and `sanitizeLogMessage("a" + string(r) + "b")` returns its input unchanged. Not a fresh reproduction of a channel — F285's three schemes are already tests — but the same arithmetic: eight code points is three bits per position | **Low, and it is bounded by a property the tree now asserts.** A covert channel needs an end to write bits and an end to read them back. The write end is open by construction, since `log` costs no permission and a module may write a secret in plain text anyway. **The read end is closed**: `log` declares no out-parameter, no ABI function hands log content back, a guest gets no preopened file and its stdout and stderr are discarded, and an add-on's storage is a schema the log does not live in — `TestAnAddonPostsToTheLogAndCannotReadItBack` drives every function in the ABI to assert it. So the channel pays out only if an operator hands the log to the add-on's author, which is a person's decision rather than a capability this host grants. What would change the tier is anything that lets a module read what it logged | **Reviewed and decided 2026-08-21** — the owner's answer on M62's fourth stop: narrow the claim, state the residue, file the channel. The row is open because the *property* that bounds it has to keep holding, and the thing to watch is any milestone that gives a module a way back into log content — a manager surface that reflects a line to an add-on, a diagnostics function, an event carrying host log text. [M68](phase-details/m68.md) is the first candidate |

| F334 | M69 | **An add-on cannot make an outbound network request, so the OIDC add-on this phase was built to prove cannot be written.** The ABI has sixteen functions and none reaches the network; the permission vocabulary has eight tokens and none names egress; `guestModuleConfig` grants a name, a start function, a random source and two clocks, and no filesystem, no sockets, no preopens. There is no arrangement of the current sixteen functions that performs it | `internal/addon/abi/functions.go` — the whole `Functions` list, none network-shaped; `internal/addon/abi/permissions.go` — eight tokens, `config.read`, `storage.own_schema`, `routes.own_prefix`, `session.context`, `session.mint`, `redirect.observe`, `redirect.inline`, `redirect.rewrite_query`; `internal/addon/host.go:169-179` (`guestModuleConfig`). `grep -rn 'http.Client\|net.Dial\|WithFS\|sock_' internal/addon --include='*.go'` outside tests returns nothing, and `docs/addon-abi.md` contains neither *outbound* nor *egress* | Found 2026-08-25 at M69's step 1, by the orchestrator, while writing the add-on's `go.mod` after the owner directed the add-on be built (D362). **This is the acceptance test working**, not failing: m69.md's own framing is *if the OIDC add-on cannot be built, the foundation is wrong*, and this is that sentence's answer arriving before a line of the add-on was written rather than after | **The highest severity this phase has produced, and it is a gap rather than a defect.** Nothing shipped is false — no document claims an add-on can reach the network — so no milestone is reopened by it. What it blocks is M69 entirely, and behind M69 the phase's remaining two rows. The fix is a new capability and it is the sharpest one this ABI could gain: **this product refuses SSRF everywhere**, at a single validator call site held by test (M30) and again at M67's *nothing is fetched from a URL*, and an add-on making outbound requests is that same risk arriving through a door the validator does not watch. Any answer has to bound it — hosts declared in the manifest and resolved at install, no redirects to private addresses, a size cap, a timeout — and that is a milestone's worth of design, not a function signature. **Owner-scheduled: the loop stopped and asked** | **Reviewed and re-homed 2026-08-25.** The owner's answer is [M68.5](phase-details/m68.5.md) — an add-on declares that it needs egress and the operator names the origins, per D364 — placed before [M69](phase-details/m69.md), which now carries a hard edge on it. This row stays open until that milestone lands and closes it. |

## Closed

A row leaves *Open* when the work that makes it untrue has landed, and the
milestone that closed it is named. The finding itself is not edited — the
evidence is what it was, and rewriting it would hide the shape of the defect
from anyone reading back.

A row also leaves *Open* when it is **merged** into another row carrying the same
defect. Merging is not deleting: the row moves here untouched, its *Closed by*
names the survivor, says what was folded in and what — if anything — was left
behind, and the survivor's *Reviewed* cell names it back. Two rows merge only
when one edit closes both. Two distinct defects that merely share a cause stay
apart and cross-reference each other instead, which is the more common answer.

| # | Found in | Finding | Where | Evidence | Severity | Reviewed | Closed by |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| F335 | M68.5 | *(**Superseded 2026-08-26, before anybody worked from it.** Filed by M68.5's worker as *the tripwire fails under coverage and fails on main too*, which is true and is not the cause. The orchestrator measured it and reopened M67 instead; what is written below is the filing, kept because a row that was acted on should say what it said. The cause is in [D368](decisions.md#2026-08-26--m67-reopened-the-leak-tripwire-measures-warm-up) and the repair is M67's.)* **`TestRepeatedInstallAndRemovalDoesNotGrowResidentMemory` fails under the coverage instrumentation `make check` runs it with, and it fails on `main` too.** The test measures this process's resident set across ten install/remove cycles against a 16 MiB tolerance, and the run is not repeatable | `internal/addon/lifecycle_test.go:625-666` and `heldBytes` at `:679` | Filed at M68.5's build, 2026-08-26. **Re-homed the same day to [M67](phase-details/m67.md)'s reopening**, which is where the repair belongs: the test is M67's and so is the bullet it fails to deliver | **Low, and the row is a pointer now rather than a finding.** Its subject is real and is being repaired under M67's number, so this stays open only until that reopening lands and closes it — leaving two trackers holding one repair is what the supersession above exists to prevent | **Reviewed 2026-08-26 by the orchestrator, and measured rather than reasoned about.** Growth against cycle count, finalizers settled with five GC/`FreeOSMemory` rounds 150ms apart: 5 cycles **+27,972 KiB**, 10 **+29,416**, 20 **+30,680**, 40 **+40,248** — per-cycle falling 5,594 → 2,941 → 1,534 → 1,006 KiB. A per-cycle leak holds per-cycle cost constant; this falls 5.5x, and ten to twenty cycles adds 1.2 MiB in total. **There is no leak.** The tripwire takes one warm-up cycle and then allows 16 MiB across the window that grows ~28 MiB in warm-up alone, so whether it passes depends on how much warm-up lands inside the measurement — which is the 20-to-103 MiB spread its filer observed | **[M67](phase-details/m67.md), reopened.** The tripwire this row is about now measures two windows of five cycles rather than a total over ten, and bounds the later one by a fraction of what the first cost rather than by a byte count nobody had measured against — so *fails under coverage* stops being a property of the instrument. Closed by the repair rather than by its own diagnosis, which was wrong and says so above |
| F326 | M66 | **The inline deadline covers instantiation, so on hardware slower than the machine it was measured on an add-on is killed before its own code runs.** `Host.Inline` and `startObserving` wrap the whole call — instantiating the module *and* invoking the guest — in one `context.WithTimeout(ctx, h.inlineDeadline)`. `DefaultInlineDeadline` is 25ms, chosen from measurements taken on an idle VM where instantiation is ~1.6ms. On a hosted CI runner under `-race` instantiation alone exceeds 25ms and **every** invocation dies at `step=instantiate`, so an inline add-on observes nothing, vetoes nothing and rewrites nothing while the redirect completes as though no add-on were installed. It is silent by design: the kill counter moves, the redirect succeeds, and nothing says the module never ran | `internal/addon/redirect.go:320` (inline) and `:510` (observe), both `context.WithTimeout(ctx, h.inlineDeadline)` around instantiation, against `DefaultInlineDeadline = 25 * time.Millisecond` at `:131` | Found by CI on 07c9ccf, not by any local gate: five `test/integration/addon_redirect_test.go` tests fail on the runner and pass on this VM — `TestAnInlineAddonThatAllowsChangesNothing` (*the module was not called*), `TestAVetoedRedirectIsRefusedAndTellsTheVisitorNothing` (302, want 403), `TestARewritingAddonChangesTheQueryAndNothingElse` (the `utm_source` survives), `TestAVetoDoesNotSpendAOneTimeLinksClick`, `TestAnObservingAddonIsFedFromTheClickPipeline`. Every failure log carries `step=instantiate`. Run: https://github.com/DevOfPie/LinkCtrl/actions/runs/32635154913 | **High, and it falsifies M66's own claims** rather than merely degrading them: the two classes do not run, so the extension point, the veto, the query rewrite and the per-module histogram are all inert on any machine slow enough. It also undermines the data D318, D319 and D325 were each decided on — the deadline's default, the decision not to pool, and the owner's confirmation of 25ms were all taken from the best case. **Owner reopened M66 on 2026-08-23** rather than scheduling a successor: the deadline is to bound the guest call, with instantiation given its own wider bound as the host's cost rather than the module's | **Owner-approved, 2026-08-23.** Reopening chosen over pooling (the other option put) — pooling removes instantiation from the request path entirely and revisits D319, and was declined as several days' work against the smallest correct fix | **M66, the same milestone, reopened rather than succeeded.** `LINKCTRL_ADDON_INSTANTIATE_DEADLINE` (500ms) bounds instantiation and `LINKCTRL_ADDON_INLINE_DEADLINE` (25ms) bounds the guest call, on both the inline and the observe path; `linkctrl_addon_redirect_kills_total` gained a `step` label so *the add-on never ran* and *the add-on declined to act* are different readings, with a different warning line each. The bound is re-read after the instance exists rather than left to wazero's cooperative interruption (D330), which also makes a hostile bound deterministic — and that is what the two new tests use to reach this defect on a machine that is not slow, one of them through a visitor end to end. Both harnesses now buy generous bounds by default so no test asserts that this VM is fast (D331). **Measuring it under contention said more than the finding did**: with all sixteen slots busy, instantiation costs a mean of 9.6ms and a worst of 62.7ms *on this machine*, so contention alone puts it past the whole 25ms deadline and F326 was never only a CI-runner problem (D328). Pooling stays declined and D319 stands, per D327. *(**Reversed 2026-08-23** by [D333](decisions.md#2026-08-23--m665-added-pooling-because-a-well-behaved-add-on-cost-4489ms): the measurement D319 was decided without — a well-behaved add-on at 44.89ms visitor p99 with 38.6% of redirects skipping it for want of a slot — is what changed the answer, and the owner added [M66.5](phase-details/m66.5.md) to pool instances. The sentence before this one was true when written; it is what the reversal reversed)* |

The half worth watching is M66's: an observation function is not a mistake an inline class can work around, and the veto is the class's whole point. *(**First half discharged 2026-08-20 at M64**, which is what this row asked for: `session_context` exists, live, costing a `session.context` grant that the vocabulary did not previously carry — D258 records why that is a seventh token rather than a fold into `routes.own_prefix`, and D258's own entry names what the cheaper answer would have cost. So `redirect_decision_read` and `redirect_answer_write` are the inline class's own pair, costing `redirect.inline`, and `redirect_event_read` went live for the observe class unchanged. **What the row could not know, and D317 decided**, is that the channel carries a query as well as a verdict; the second grant that costs is `redirect.rewrite_query`. | F284 | M64 | **Cross-site unsafe methods to an add-on's routes are 403, and it is not conditional on credentials — so an OIDC `response_mode=form_post` callback is refused while a server-to-server webhook passes.** `http.CrossOriginProtection` refuses *every* cross-site unsafe request, not only a cookie-authenticated one, and M64 mounted add-on routes inside the application chain that carries it. A form-post callback is a browser navigation from the identity provider's origin, so it arrives cross-site and never reaches the module; a webhook from a server sends no `Sec-Fetch-Site` and reaches it. **`same-site` is refused too**, so a provider on a sibling subdomain of the dashboard is refused as well. Not false for any M64 bullet — the routes work, the middleware is right, and the milestone is not the one that builds a callback — which is why it is a row. **Addressed to [M65](phase-details/m65.md)**, which builds an authentication flow on exactly this path and therefore has to answer it: the choices are a trusted-origin registration an add-on's manifest declares, an exemption bounded to a declared callback path, a `code`-flow-only stance that never posts, or nothing, deliberately. **[M69](phase-details/m69.md)'s acceptance test is where an unanswered version of this surfaces** — the OIDC add-on is the first real consumer, so a 403 discovered there arrives in the milestone least able to absorb it, after M65, M67 and M68 have all been built on the assumption that a callback lands | `internal/httpx/router.go` — the add-on patterns are registered on `appMux`, whose chain in `NewRouter` applies `http.NewCrossOriginProtection().Handler(appHandler)` to everything on it; the comment beside the registration was corrected in M64's own diff, where it had claimed the middleware applies *"to a cookie-authenticated unsafe method"*. `internal/addon/http.go` is unreached | Measured through `NewRouter` with a real module behind the route, POST to `/addons/probe/callback`: `Sec-Fetch-Site: cross-site` **403**, `same-site` **403**, `same-origin` **200**, `none` **200**, header absent **200**. The module was not entered in either 403 | **Medium, and it is scheduling rather than a defect.** The behaviour is the correct default and the cost is that the surface M64 exists to enable has one shape it cannot serve. Whichever way M65 answers it, the answer is a change to what a cross-origin request may do to this product, so it is the owner's | **Unreviewed.** | **[M65](phase-details/m65.md)**, 2026-08-21, by [D291](decisions.md#2026-08-21--m65-the-callback-arrives-as-a-redirect-and-the-guest-gets-a-real-random-source) — **the stance, and no exemption.** The row's four options were: a trusted-origin registration the manifest declares, an exemption bounded to a declared callback path, a `code`-flow-only stance, or nothing deliberately. The owner took the third. Nothing is exempted from the cross-site refusal, so there is no carve-out to get wrong — the argument that decided it was that this phase had just spent four workers watching carve-outs leak in M62 — and the two declined options are recorded in the entry rather than left as *we decided this did not matter*. **The cost is published in the two places the row's disposition implies and the decision required**: `docs/addon-abi.md` has a section addressed to the publisher (*Your callback arrives as a redirect*), naming the measured statuses and saying that a provider offering only `form_post` cannot be used, and `docs/configuration.md`'s *When an add-on signs people in* says the same thing to the operator. The behaviour is unchanged — nothing in the middleware moved and no test of it was touched — which is what makes this a closure by decision rather than by work. **What M65 added on top of the row's own scope** is the consequence of a GET callback nobody had written down: the cross-site refusal does not reach a GET, so an add-on's callback carries its own CSRF exposure and the `state` parameter is the add-on's to get right. The ABI page says so at `identity_link` and in the same section, because the host's guarantee — a link is only ever made for whoever is signed in, in their own browser, now — is narrower than a publisher would assume from the rest of the boundary. [M69](phase-details/m69.md)'s provider is still to be checked against `form_post` **before** M69, which is the row's own instruction and is not this milestone's to discharge. |

What survives is the **composition**: the isolation property and the fake-RNG property together are worse than either alone | **High in effect, known in kind.** An OIDC add-on is the stated first consumer, and its `state`, `nonce` and PKCE verifier would each be one constant shared by every LinkCtrl instance on earth, with `exp` checks running against 2022. The dispositions are: the ABI grows a real random source and clock before M65; or `sdk/doc.go` and `docs/addon-abi.md` say the constant out loud, since *not what they are on a server* reads as a bad clock rather than as a fixed OAuth state parameter | **Unreviewed.** | **[M65](phase-details/m65.md)**, 2026-08-21, by [D292](decisions.md#2026-08-21--m65-the-callback-arrives-as-a-redirect-and-the-guest-gets-a-real-random-source) and [D293](decisions.md#2026-08-21--m65-what-d292-costs-a-publisher-and-four-host-rules-nobody-asked-for-by-name). **The repair is underneath `crypto/rand` and `time.Now`, not in front of them**, which is the disposition the row did not offer and is what the owner's *the ABI grows a real random source and a real clock* turned out to require: `guestModuleConfig` sets `WithRandSource(rand.Reader)`, `WithSysWalltime()` and `WithSysNanotime()`, which feed WASI's `random_get` and `clock_time_get` — the calls a wasip1 guest's standard library makes. So an add-on built against the older SDK, which cannot call a function that did not exist when it was compiled, gets real entropy and a real clock with no rebuild. The ABI's own `random_bytes` and `time_now` land beside them, ungated, as the documented spelling of the same two sources; the ABI moves 0.1.0 → **0.1.1**, a patch, because adding a function is additive and under `0.x` the minor is the breaking axis (D294). **The row's second disposition was declined**: `sdk/doc.go` and `docs/addon-abi.md` now say the opposite of what they said rather than saying the constant out loud, because *the fake must stop being reachable in a way a publisher can mistake for a real one* and `crypto/rand.Read` is exactly that reachability. **Both halves were needed and that is measured, not argued**: sabotaged by restoring wazero's defaults at the per-request site alone, `random_bytes` kept working while the guest's own `crypto/rand` returned identical bytes across two separately-opened hosts and `time.Now` answered `2022-01-01T00:00:00Z`. **The enumeration of instantiation sites is closed structurally** — `TestOnlyOneModuleConfigIsBuilt` sweeps this package's non-test sources for `wazero.NewModuleConfig(` and fails on anything but `guestModuleConfig`, so M67's lifecycle work cannot add a third site that quietly gets the defaults. The behavioural assertion is `TestEveryRequestDrawsItsOwnEntropy`, which draws across three axes the compile-time constant made identical — two requests to one host, and a second independently-opened host — for both the ABI's function and the guest's own `crypto/rand`, and checks both clocks against 2023 rather than against this machine being right. Its Severity cell called the fix *a context with a timeout around instantiation and a class to put the expiry in*; that is what landed, plus the scoping D273 needed and the compile correction D274 needed. The fix is a `statement_timeout` on the migration connection, and the number is a decision: too tight and a legitimate `CREATE INDEX` on a large table fails an upgrade, which is the case M63 chose five minutes for | No | **Merged into [F274](#open)**, M60, 2026-08-20 ([D275](decisions.md#2026-08-20--m60-one-defect-gets-one-row)). This row was written from the other end, at M60's reopening, as the consequence D273's scoping leaves behind, and nobody checked whether the thing being described already had a number. Everything here is folded into F274 — the D273 framing, the `pg_sleep` shape, and the observation that this is the one piece of an add-on's own code the load budget does not bound — and nothing is left behind. | F286 | M64.9 | **Duplicate and case-variant JSON keys defeat both the `schema_version` equality check and the permission declaration, so a manifest deceives every reader but the host.** `encoding/json` takes the **last** occurrence of a repeated key and matches struct tags case-insensitively when no exact match exists, so `SCHEMA_VERSION` and `Permissions` bind the same fields the documented lowercase spellings do. Falsifies `phase-details/m60.md:32` and `phase-details/m62.md:41-43` | **Reproduced through the real `Open` over a real installed directory, twice independently.** Loaded as `name=sneaky schema_version=1 grants=[config.read routes.own_prefix session.mint storage.own_schema]`. Battery: permissions listed then emptied is accepted with no grants; emptied then listed is accepted holding all three; `schema_version` 99 then 1 is accepted while 1 then 99 is refused; every field spelled in uppercase is accepted. **Nothing covers the manifest bytes** — the `sha256` field is the digest of the *module* (`internal/addon/host.go:668-671`) and `MigrationFile.SHA256` covers migration files (`internal/addon/migrations.go:89-92`); there is no canonicalization and no signature over `addon.json`. `ReadManifest` has exactly two call sites and everything downstream consumes the parsed struct, so the info gauge and the boot log report the truth. `manifest_test.go:86-88` covers a permission repeated *inside* the array and `:181` covers unknown fields; nothing covers a repeated key | **Medium-high.** Not a sandbox escape — an operator reading the boot log is not fooled. The deceived party is whoever reads the **file**, which is the artifact another repository is compiled against and the thing a reviewer inspects before installing. Both of the manifest's strictest rules are bypassable by a publisher | Yes — owner approved 2026-08-20 on M64.9's triage; **reopens [M60](phase-details/m60.md)**. Fix is self-contained: re-decode into `map[string]json.RawMessage` or walk the decoder's token stream in `parseManifest`, refusing exact repeats and case-collisions | **[M60](phase-details/m60.md)**, 2026-08-20, the reopening D269 scheduled ([D271](decisions.md#2026-08-20--m60-a-manifest-means-what-it-reads-as)). `parseManifest` now walks the document's token stream against the Go type it decodes into and refuses two things: a key not spelled exactly as `docs/configuration.md` documents it, and a key that appears more than once. **Taken further than the row's fix note**, which said *exact repeats and case-collisions*: a lone `"Permissions"` with no lowercase twin is refused too, because the reasoning behind `DisallowUnknownFields` — a key this host does not know was written for a schema it does not implement — applies to spelling, and a second accepted spelling of every field is a second way to write the format `LinkCtrl-OIDC` compiles against. **Recursive**, so a `migrations` entry carrying `file` twice is refused as well; only the top level was reproduced, but that one names one `.sql` to a reader and hashes another. The key set is read from the struct tags rather than listed, so a field added later is covered without anybody remembering. The row's other observation — that nothing hashes `addon.json` — stands and is why exactness is the answer rather than tolerance; signing the manifest is M69's provenance question. Cost: the file is read into memory to be walked twice, so the read is bounded at 64 KiB — `maxManifestBytes`, which is its own constant and not the ABI's `maxStringIn`: the two are the same number for unrelated contracts, and aliasing them would have let a change made for an ABI reason move what manifests this host accepts. Documented in `docs/configuration.md`'s manifest section, because meeting an undocumented bound as a boot failure is the same experience as a bug. Sabotage, all restored by counter-edit: bypassing the check parses **10** cases, dropping the duplicate rule fails exactly the four repeat cases, dropping the recursion fails exactly the two nested ones, dropping the bound fails its own test |
| F287 | M64.9 | **Nothing bounds load-time instantiation, so a `degrade`-class add-on hangs boot forever instead of being skipped.** The host instantiates with the context `Open` was given, and that context carries no deadline — so a module spinning in `init()` stops the instance ever listening, with no log line naming it, no metric and no error. The same gap gives an unbounded load-time log flood, since `log` is ungated, only one line's size is bounded, and `Open` will not return to stop it. | `internal/addon/host.go:719-723` instantiates with `Open`'s context; `cmd/linkctrl/main.go:172` is `signal.NotifyContext(context.Background(), …)` with no deadline, passed at `:280` **before the listener**. Falsifies `phase-details/m60.md:44-46` — *logs, increments a metric, and the instance serves without it* | **Reproduced twice.** A `degrade`-class fixture spinning in `init()`: `Open` did not return within 20 s, and the only log line emitted was the guest's own. Compilation does not catch it — the spin is at instantiation — and there is no timeout in `loadOne` or its caller. **The runtime is already configured correctly**: `WithCloseOnContextDone(true)` is set, and with a 3 s deadline on `Open`'s context the second reproduction returned in 3.0023 s and logged `m60.md:44-46`'s contract verbatim, *add-on failed to load; the instance continues without it … failure_class=degrade error="module closed with context deadline exceeded"*. So the defect is one missing deadline and not a missing mechanism. Log flood measured at **3697 lines / 14.7 MiB in 2 s**. SIGTERM does cancel the hang, so *forever* is *until an operator kills it*; a boot that needs SIGTERM is still a hang | **Medium-high.** Falsifies a milestone bullet that is asserted by test for every other failure mode, and the failure is silent — nothing says which add-on boot is stuck on | Yes — owner approved 2026-08-20 on M64.9's triage; **reopens [M60](phase-details/m60.md)**. The fix is one `context.WithTimeout` around the call handed to `addon.Open`, and it has already been shown to produce the exact documented behaviour | **[M60](phase-details/m60.md)**, 2026-08-20, the reopening D269 scheduled ([D272](decisions.md#2026-08-20--m60-one-add-ons-load-is-bounded-and-the-budget-is-its-own)). `Open` gives each add-on's load a context of its own with a **30 s** deadline, and a ninth outcome `load_timeout` distinguishes *never returned* from *trapped*. **Per add-on rather than for the whole load**, which the row's fix note did not decide: a shared budget once expired is expired for every add-on behind the slow one, so one `degrade` module's hang would refuse a healthy `required` one and stop the instance — the very bullet this reopening repairs. Asserted, not argued: with a shared budget the test fails as `add-on "zfine": load_timeout`. 30 s is the number `TestInstantiationCostIsMeasured` has called the boundary since M60 shipped, and that test now reads the constant so the two cannot drift; one 1.87 MB fixture loads in 388 ms, measured 2026-08-20. The hazard the fix introduces is asserted too — `WithCloseOnContextDone` would close a healthy module if its load context outlived the load — by calling into a loaded add-on after its budget passed. **Sabotage: removing the deadline does not fail the test, it hangs it**, `panic: test timed out after 25s`, which is F287 reproduced through the shipped suite. **The log flood this row also names is bounded and not fixed** — 30 s of an ungated `log` is still roughly 200 MiB — and is [F305](#open), because log volume is no claim of `m60.md`'s. **Scoped at the second attempt** ([D273](decisions.md#2026-08-20--m60-what-the-load-budget-bounds-is-the-add-ons-own-code)): the budget is given to `CompileModule` and `InstantiateModule` and to nothing else, because laying it over the whole of `loadOne` also caps the five-minute migration-lock wait M63 chose to prevent a crash loop — this row's fix re-breaking a shipped milestone from the other side. What the budget therefore does not bound is [F274](#open). **Corrected again at the third attempt** ([D274](decisions.md#2026-08-20--m60-the-compile-step-was-bounded-by-nothing)): the compile half was wrapped in `runGuest` and enforced by nothing. wazero checks the context while compiling only on its multi-worker path, and the single-worker branch it takes by default walks the code section with no check in it — so a compile past the budget returned a nil error, expiry is read only when a step errors, and the load simply finished late while four documents said it was bounded. `compileWorkers` is now on the context `CompileModule` runs under, and `TestTheCompileStepIsBounded` is red without it. **[F267](#closed) is this row filed two days earlier** and is closed by the same work |
| F291 | M64.9 | **The gate M59 built to close F248 reports present rows as missing and exits 1 on a clean tree.** Its row-membership pass reintroduces the `cmd` piped into `grep -q` shape under `set -uo pipefail` — `grep -q` exits at the first match, the writer upstream takes SIGPIPE, and `pipefail` reports 141 for a pipeline that succeeded. **The same file forbids the pattern in a comment and avoids it in its first pass**, which uses a herestring and has no upstream process to kill. | `scripts/check-links.sh:230` and `:239`, under `set -uo pipefail` at `:44`, feeding `exit 1` at `:326-327`. With background busy loops on 8 cores: 0 loops, 20 runs, 0 failures; 6 loops, 4 runs, **4 failures**, 4–6 false FAILs each; 12 loops, 8 runs, **8 failures**, 10–148 false FAILs each. Every FAIL is false — rows named as missing return exactly 1 from `grep -cxF` against the same cache. **The refuter found the mechanism the finder had not**: `/usr/bin/cat` on this machine is uutils coreutils 0.8.0, which `splice()`s file to pipe, and `strace` shows the payload transferring in one call and the fault arriving on the **second, EOF-probing** `splice()` — `PIPESTATUS=[141 0]` in 1262 of 5000 iterations under 16-way load. Size is irrelevant, so small caches are no protection; a read/write `cat` never touches the pipe at EOF, scoring 0/500 where `/usr/bin/cat` scored 8/500 in the same loop | **High for the process gate, none for the product.** It fails one direction only — SIGPIPE requires an early match, so a genuinely missing row still reports correctly and no wrong link can pass. The threshold is low: six background loops on eight cores, which is an ordinary parallel-build or multi-agent machine. **Scoped to this machine rather than to CI** — GitHub runners ship GNU coreutils, whose `cat` is read/write, so `make ci-build` is a real caller that is not expected to exhibit it. What it bites is `make check` and `make check-links` locally, which is where the loop's own gates run | Yes — owner approved 2026-08-20 on M64.9's triage; **reopens [M59](phase-details/m59.md)**. The fix is the idiom already in the file — `grep -qxF "$key" "$file"` directly, or the herestring form pass 1 uses | **[M59](phase-details/m59.md)**, 2026-08-20. `row_present` greps the cache file directly, so nothing is piped into `grep -q` and no writer is left to kill — the idiom the same file already prescribed at `:102-105`. Reproduced before it was fixed, on the tree as shipped: eight background busy loops on eight cores, **6 of 6 runs red**, 65 to 211 false FAIL lines each; the same load after the fix, 8 of 8 green, and 8 of 8 again at twelve loops. Sabotage put back both branches — a link moved to the wrong table and a row identifier no table holds — and the gate named each correctly, two FAILs and no others, four runs out of four under the same load |
| F266 | M60 | **The two fixture builders disagree about when a WASM module is stale, so `make test` can run against a module `task test` would have rebuilt.** The Makefile's pattern rule makes each `.wasm` depend on its module's `main.go` **alone**, while its recipe compiles the whole package directory — so a fixture module that grows a second `.go` file is still built from that file and is no longer rebuilt when it changes. The Taskfile's `addon-fixtures` runs `go build` over every module unconditionally, so the mirror cannot go stale at all. Two builders, two staleness rules, one target name. **Same class as open [F259](#open)** — the Taskfile mirror running something weaker than the Makefile under the same name — and two of them now argue the mirror needs one mechanism rather than two hand-maintained lists | Makefile: `grep -n 'ADDON_FIXTURE' Makefile` gives the `ADDON_FIXTURES` wildcard over `*/main.go`, the `$(ADDON_FIXTURE_DIR)/%.wasm: $(ADDON_FIXTURE_SRC)/%/main.go` pattern rule, and the recipe that builds `./$(ADDON_FIXTURE_SRC)/$*` — a directory, not the prerequisite. Taskfile: `grep -n 'addon-fixtures' Taskfile.yml` gives the task whose `cmds` loop rebuilds every module each run, and `grep -n 'sources:' Taskfile.yml` returns **nothing**, so no task in the mirror uses Task's own up-to-date mechanism. The reader that consumes whatever is on disk without asking is `fixture()` — `grep -n 'os.ReadFile(path)' internal/addon/host_test.go` | Read from both files on 2026-08-18 while splitting the cost measurement, which is what made the pattern rule's single prerequisite visible. **Not driven, and not drivable today**: both fixture modules are one `main.go` each — `ls internal/addon/testdata/modules/*/` — so nothing exists for the divergence to bite on, which is also why it would arrive as a stale artifact rather than as an error. What is verified is the asymmetry itself: make rebuilt nothing when only a non-`main.go` input could have changed, because there is no such input for it to depend on | **Low, and it fails as a green run against old bytes.** The failure is a load that succeeds against yesterday's module, or a digest mismatch, in the one package whose whole subject is verifying that bytes are the bytes a manifest describes — so the flake reads as the feature working, exactly as [F264](#open) does. The fix is not symmetrical, which is why it is a row: a pattern rule cannot take a `$(wildcard)` over its own stem, so the Makefile side is either a per-module prerequisite list or an order-only dependency on the directory, while the Taskfile side is one `sources:`/`generates:` pair. Whether the mirror should carry two hand-maintained lists at all is F259's question, and the two want answering together | **Unreviewed.** | **Closed by [M61](phase-details/m61.md)**, under [phase-loop.md](phase-loop.md#1-validate)'s deferred-overlap rule rather than by owner approval: M61's own bullet says the M60 test modules *"are rebuilt on top of [the SDK], which is the first proof the SDK compiles a working consumer"*, and under a rule watching `main.go` alone a change to the **SDK** does not rebuild a fixture — so that proof could be a stale artifact. The exception is named in the commit message. Fixed in all three places rather than two, because the divergence stopped being theoretical the moment a shared input existed: the Makefile's pattern rule takes `.SECONDEXPANSION:` and a `$(wildcard)` over its own stem plus every `sdk/*.go`, which the row's own fix note said a pattern rule could not do; the Taskfile's task takes the first `sources:`/`generates:` pair in that file; and `fixture()` compares the same mtimes, because the release workflow and the CI image job reach it without a make target at all (F262). **Driven on the way in, not after**: the two-add-on scoping test failed against a `probe.wasm` built before an edit to its own source, which is exactly the stale artifact this row predicted, reported as a guest panic. **F259's question is not answered** — the mirror still carries hand-maintained lists, and now three of them rather than two. |

| F256 | M58 *(reopened)* | **The single-instance conformance check breaks its readiness wait on Postgres's *temporary* server, then confirms against the gap that follows it.** `scripts/single-instance-check.sh:93-96` loops until `pg_isready` succeeds and **breaks**; `:97` then re-runs it once as a confirmation and fails the whole check if it says no. Those are two different servers. `postgres:17-alpine` runs `initdb` against a temporary server on first boot, which answers `pg_isready`, then shuts it down and starts the real one — so the loop can break on the temporary server and the confirming line can land in the window where nothing is listening. Whether it does is decided by where the one-second cadence falls, which is why it is a coin flip in CI and never fails here | `scripts/single-instance-check.sh:93-98`, invoked as `single-instance` from `Makefile:414` and run by CI's `Container image` job | **Measured, and it corrects this row's own first version, which said timing rather than code.** Polling `pg_isready` continuously from container start against `postgres:17-alpine`: `................RRR.....RRRRRRRR…` — ready for three polls, **not-ready for five**, then ready permanently. One `R` to `.` transition, which is the temporary server going down. It reproduced on both runs of the same commit and in opposite directions: `32078341466` failed and `32079716931` passed on `5e0fb30`, then `32081422004` failed and `32081417936` passed on `fe4791e`. The naive double-check could not be reproduced as a failure on this machine over six attempts, and that is consistent rather than contradictory — the loop breaks after two attempts here, so the sleep cadence clears the gap | **Medium, and it is the gate rather than the product.** Nothing about the product is wrong and the check has never once failed for the reason it exists. But it is the gate M57's *a single container is still a supported configuration* rests on, it blocks the release PR, and a gate that answers differently twice for one tree teaches everyone to re-run it — which is how a gate stops being one. The fix is to require the ready state to **hold** rather than to appear once: consecutive successes inside the loop, with no separate confirming shot after it | **Unreviewed.** | **Owner-scheduled 2026-08-18**, outside any milestone — *the phase/PR cannot be considered complete unless all tests succeed appropriately* — so it lands as a task-class commit under the scope gate rather than by reopening a `done` row. **Fixed by asking over TCP instead of the container's unix socket**, which removes the race rather than widening the wait: the entrypoint runs the `initdb` temporary server with `listen_addresses=''`, so it has no TCP listener and cannot be seen. Measured both ways from container start — socket `........RR...RRRR`, TCP `............RRRRR`, **zero** ready-to-not-ready transitions on TCP. It is also the honest question, since the container under test reaches Postgres across a docker network and never over that socket. A three-success streak stays as belt and is documented as belt: a first attempt used a streak **alone** and was caught by measurement, because one run's temporary server answered ready for exactly three consecutive polls. There is no check after the loop; a single shot outside it was the defect. **Sabotage-verified against the new mechanism** — the real server started with `listen_addresses=''` makes the check refuse after its budget with *Postgres never became ready*, where the old code passed on the socket; restored by counter-edit, byte-identical |

The rule is owner-answered: **the highest level that does not grow the symbol**, so correction is taken whenever it is free, and H while a logo is drawn | **Closed by [M50.6](phase-details/m50.6.md)'s second reopening, 2026-08-13.** The level is a rule now — the strongest one that does not push the symbol into a larger version — and since [D187](decisions.md) it is a **floor under every code** rather than a default for codes that named nothing: the drawn level is the stronger of what the row names and what the rule gives, so `H` is kept where it is needed and nobody, including an API caller, draws less correction than costs nothing. `ClearQRCodeLogo` clears the level with the bytes and re-fits the size against the smaller symbol, in one statement, so a code whose logo leaves goes back to the level its own payload earns rather than staying at the H the upload forced; `TestRemovingALogoRecomputesTheLevelRatherThanKeepingH` reads the row and not only the view. *(**The population this closure does not reach, named at step 3.4 rather than left to be discovered**: a row whose logo was removed by a build older than this one kept its `H`, and a stored `H` binds under the floor, so nothing would recompute it. QR logos have never shipped — `03800_qr_code_logo.sql` landed 2026-08-07, after `v0.2.0`, and 0.3.0 is untagged — so no instance an operator can have holds one, and neither dev instance does: zero rows at `H` with a null logo on demo and on test, checked 2026-08-13. Raised by this milestone's reviewer, which read the closure as unqualified.)* The Where cell's three `internal/link/qr.go` sites are the forcing, which is unchanged and correct. **The half nobody had noticed is that the same `M` was pinned on every code that never had a logo**: `Normalize` wrote the default into every style this product stored, and it stops — but nothing is migrated, because under the floor a stored `M` cannot bind and resolves exactly as an unset one does. `CreateQRCode` no longer copies a logo-forced `H` onto the code it creates, which was this finding rebuilt inside its own fix. Nothing changes size where the floor cannot raise the symbol, which `TestTheRuleNeverChangesTheModuleCount` sweeps the version range for; where it can, every site that fits a size encodes through the rule, and the one row shape that predates that is [F232](#open). The tab still does not print the level; it names it where a reader's own action moves it (D186) |
| F226 | M50 *(reopened)* | **[F225](#closed)'s defect moved onto the default code, and the measurement in that row no longer describes it.** F225 is *creating a second code copies the default's stored size onto a longer payload*; after D183 both payloads are the same length, because the default gains an eight-character slug in the same operation — so the copy is exact and the created code is fine. What grows instead is **the default's own payload**, against a `size` fitted before it had a tag. A default code stored at its own floor therefore draws larger than the number the reader set, on the code they set it on | Measured 2026-08-13 in `internal/link` against alias `summer`: untagged payload **29 modules**, floor `qr.MinSizeFor(29)` = **70px** at scale 2; tagged payload **33 modules**, floor **78px** at that scale. A style stored at 70/scale 2 draws 70px before the create and **82px** after it, because `fitGeometry` falls back to margin-and-scale once the symbol and its quiet zone no longer fit inside `Style.Size`. `internal/link/qr.go`, `CreateQRCode` — the `NameQRCode` call that gives the default a slug does not re-fit the row it names | Found by measurement while building M50's reopening, filed rather than fixed. **Out of spec**: every bullet of the reopening is about identity, removability, promotion and what an already-printed picture resolves to, and none of them is about a stored size. It is also not a new class — it is F225's, one code to the left, which is why it is a row pointing at that one rather than a second description of the same thing | **Low, and lower than F225's.** It needs a code stored at or very near its own floor, which is the bottom of the slider, and the picture comes out larger rather than unreadable — the fallback draws `DefaultMargin`, so the quiet zone is the ISO four modules and it still scans. The reason it is a row is that it makes [M49](phase-details/m49.md)'s *the requested size is the size stored and drawn, exactly* false in a case M49 could not have known about, and the fix is the same three-way decision F225 names: re-fit the row when it is named, refuse to name it, or say that a stored size is fitted to a payload and a payload can change | **Approved as work 2026-08-13** with F225, in the same owner answer and by the same sentence — one rule answers both, which is what that answer gave. Raised at [M50](phase-details/m50.md)'s reopening 2026-08-13. Dispositioning it should probably decide F225 at the same time — one rule answers both | **Closed by [M49](phase-details/m49.md)'s third reopening, 2026-08-13.** The half F225's fix does not reach: the default code's own row is re-fitted by `refitStoredQRCode`, written through the upsert's conflict branch so the re-fit cannot rename the code or move the flag. Asked for on **every** create rather than only where `NameQRCode` runs — a row this release inherits is already in this state, and the guard means a row that is already fitted writes nothing. The measurement in this row is the fixture — 29 modules to 33, floor 70 to 78, 82px drawn before — and it is asserted forward rather than described. Same rule, same silence, same notice as F225: D185 |
| F225 | M49 *(reopened)* | **Creating a second code copies the default's stored size onto a longer payload, so the new code draws at a size nobody set.** A named code's content carries `&qrc=<slug>` on top of the default's, which can push it into the next symbol version; the copied `size` is then below what the larger symbol needs at the copied `scale`, and `fitGeometry` falls back to the margin-and-scale arithmetic | `internal/link/qr.go`, `CreateQRCode` — it reads the default's style through `storedQRStyle` and marshals it whole, `size` included. The fallback is `internal/qr/qr.go`'s `fitGeometry`, which draws `Style.Size` only while the symbol and its quiet zone fit inside it | Measured 2026-08-13 in `internal/link`: alias `summer` gives a 29-module default code and a 33-module named one. The size control's smallest offer for the default is its own floor, `qr.MinSizeFor(29)` = **70px**, which fits at scale 2. Create a second code and that style draws **82px** — the named symbol's floor at scale 2 is 78. Longer aliases do not reach it, because both payloads land on the same version: `summer-campaign-2026` and a 40-character alias both come out equal | **Low.** It takes a code stored at or near its own floor, which is the bottom of the slider, and the picture is larger rather than unreadable — the quiet zone the fallback draws is `DefaultMargin`, so it still scans. It is a row rather than nothing because it is the same class as the defect this milestone's second reopening exists to fix: a stored size that is not the size drawn. **Out of spec here**: every bullet of the reopening is about the size somebody *requested*, and nobody requests this one — it is inherited. The fix is a decision rather than a patch, and there are three — re-fit the copied style against the new code's own symbol, drop `size` from the copy so the new code takes the default geometry, or refuse the create — which is why it is not taken here | **Approved as work 2026-08-13**, in the owner's five answers taken between milestones — *"Re-fit the row whenever the payload changes"*, with the floor allowed to move and the notice owed only where the size must rise. It reopened [M49](phase-details/m49.md) a third time rather than being taken from [M50.7](phase-details/m50.7.md), the owner choosing the milestone that made the claim over the cheaper route. Of the three fixes this row names, the owner took the first | **Closed by [M49](phase-details/m49.md)'s third reopening, 2026-08-13.** `CreateQRCode` re-fits the style it copies against the created code's *own* payload rather than marshalling the default's whole, so `size` is the size that code draws. `link.refitForPayload` keeps the number wherever the larger symbol admits a scale — 512px on the measured payload keeps 512 and moves the scale 14 → 12 — and raises it to `qr.MinSizeFor` where none does, 70 → 78 on this row's own measurement. Reported then and silent otherwise (D185), through `?qr=added&from=&to=` on the dashboard and `refit` on the create's JSON. Asserted by `TestASizeSurvivesThePayloadGrowingUnderIt` on the arithmetic and `TestAddingACodeReFitsEveryRowItsPayloadChanged` at the call site, the second reading the served SVG's `width` rather than recomputing it |

(b) `partials/link_qr.html:319` → `internal/httpx/web_qr.go:192-199` → `internal/link/qr.go:722-740` → `campaigns.sql:161-164`, `DELETE … AND slug = ''` | Owner-reported 2026-08-12 for (a); (b) found by survey the same day and verified against the SQL. (a) falsifies [M50](phase-details/m50.md)'s claim about what the default code is: it stops being *the row with the empty slug* and becomes a settable property, so an untagged scan attributes to whichever code carries the flag. **`ClickCodeParam` is already `qrc`** (`internal/domain/attribution.go:90`), so nothing is renamed and nothing already printed changes meaning — a printed picture with no `qrc` keeps attributing to the default, which starts as the code it always was. (b) is reachable by any reader with two codes and `links.update` | **Moderate.** (a) reaches the data model, the redirect's attribution, the API and the analytics breakdown, and it is the largest piece the owner asked for. (b) is a plain defect that silently discards a style the reader did not mean to clear, with no confirmation and no undo | **Approved as work 2026-08-12**, owner-chosen, into [M50](phase-details/m50.md)'s reopening. (a) The default's identity moved off the empty slug and onto `qr_codes.is_default` (`04400`, D183): every code with a sibling carries a `qrc` tag, any code can be made the default with `PUT …/qr/codes/{slug}/default`, any code can be removed while a link has more than one, and removing the flag-holder promotes the **oldest** code left and says so.
