The caller needs `links.create` or `links.update`, so this is not reachable anonymously — but on an instance with open signup (M29) or many members, a holder of an ordinary editor role can loop `POST /api/v1/links` with `http://127.0.0.1/` and add a row per request, each carrying a metadata blob and up to 2 KiB of defanged URL. Nothing rate-limits the write beyond `LINKCTRL_API_RATE_PER_MIN` (600/min by default), and unlike the other eleven audit actions this one records something that did *not* happen, so there is no successful state change bounding how often it can be provoked. | Minor, and bounded rather than unbounded: M21 shipped `LINKCTRL_AUDIT_RETENTION_DAYS` and `LINKCTRL_AUDIT_SIZE_WARN_BYTES`, so growth is trimmed and an operator is warned — but retention defaults to `0` (keep forever, D5), which is the configuration where this costs disk indefinitely. The trail is worth more than the risk, which is why the bullet is right and this is a row rather than a design change; the candidate fixes — a per-actor rate on the record, or collapsing repeated identical refusals into one row with a count — are both behaviour changes to a shipped audit contract and belong to whoever schedules them. | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 3, minor — a principal can act on it, in scope by the 2026-08-04 standing rule. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. `recordBlocked` is bounded per actor **and per refusal code**, ten a minute (`BlockedAuditRatePerMin`), through the same limiter set every other limit is built in — shared via Redis so the bound means one thing across replicas, falling back to local buckets, which errs toward more rows rather than fewer. **The per-code half is the fix, not a refinement**: a per-actor budget would be a suppression tool, because the attacker picks the noise and could bury the refusal that mattered under a flood of a different one. A code nobody has provoked before is always recorded. The bound is on the *row* and never on the block — a destination is refused identically whether or not its audit row is written, and the test asserts that too. Of the two candidates this row named, collapsing repeats into one row with a count was rejected: `audit_logs` is append-only by design and mutating a written row is a larger break of that contract than declining to add one. **[M30](phase-details/m30.md)'s bullet is amended, not silently falsified** — it read *Every blocked attempt writes an audit event*, and the amendment carries the bullet as it stood, as amended, and the tree fact. `docs/SECURITY.md`'s audit row says the same in operator terms. Sabotaged twice: keying per actor alone fails the burying assertion, and removing the bound writes twelve rows for twelve identical refusals. |
| F24 | M32.9 | The dashboard shows two different numbers for the same link's clicks, side by side | Recent-links row reads `links.click_count`, which counts bot hits; the Clicks tile reads `workspace_click_daily`, which counts them separately | Found while adversarially verifying that the dashboard scopes by workspace (it does). With 7 human and 2 bot clicks on one link, the row read 9 and the tile read 7, on the same page for the same workspace. Not a scoping defect and not wrong data — two defensible definitions of *clicks* rendered adjacently with nothing distinguishing them | Minor. A plausible source of somebody reporting that the dashboard numbers look wrong | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 6, minor — wrong behaviour nothing can provoke. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. Both figures are labelled rather than made equal, because both are correct and the dashboard needs both: the tile is the 30-day human figure the page is about, and the per-link number is the all-time total the sort order and the links page use. **The two differ on two axes, not the one this row names** — bots *and* the window — so labelling only the bot half would have left a reader with a different unexplained gap. *Recent links* now says *all time, bots included* beside the heading, each per-link count carries the full reason in its title, and the *Clicks* tile gains a marker saying bots are counted separately and the per-link totals are larger by design. No number moved: this is F107's answer applied to the same shape of problem — two defensible definitions rendered next to each other, where the defect is the silence between them rather than either figure. |
| F107 | M44.9 | The per-destination breakdown credits split-arm clicks to the link's own destination for up to fifteen minutes, and always reports zero visitors for that row | `internal/analytics/reader.go:245-253` computes the primary row as `totalClicks - attributed`, where the total comes from a 60-second rollup and the attribution from the 15-minute rollup M37 introduced; `primary` is built at `:215-219` without `UniqueVisitors` and only `.Clicks` is assigned at `:251` | The comment licensing the clamp says the two figures *"can leave the totals a few clicks apart"* and was written at M36, before M37 split the cadence; the gap is now a quarter-hour of traffic and the shortfall always lands on the primary row. `internal/ui/templates/pages/link_detail.html:667` presents the column as *"attributed to the destination that served them"*. Worst case is a split test launched today and viewed inside its first fifteen minutes: 100% to the link's own destination, 0% to the arms. `Plan.md:826` records that the breakdown *lags*; it does not record that the lag is rendered as positive attribution to a named row. The zero-visitors defect is permanent rather than transient | **Minor to moderate**, restated 2026-08-04 (M45) from *Minor, upper end*, which described only the transient half. Wrong numbers on the one panel a split test is read from, worst at the moment somebody is most likely to look. The clamp's error self-corrects within fifteen minutes; the zero-visitors column does not correct at all, and the row's own evidence says so — a figure that is permanently wrong under a heading reading *"attributed to the destination that served them"* is a wrong number rather than a late one, and nothing on the panel distinguishes the two. | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 2, minor to moderate. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05, owner-directed: *keep the row, mark it approximate*. The remainder is still shown — it is the best figure available — but it no longer claims the clicks were attributed to the link's own destination. `Approximate` is set while the destination rollup is behind, and conditioned on `attributed > 0` so an ordinary link running no split test does not carry the caveat. `VisitorsKnown` is false on that row **always** rather than only during the lag: unique visitors are counted per destination and this is the row the rollup never writes, so `0` was a measurement nobody took. The template renders *approximate* beside the count and an em dash for visitors, with the reason in each title, and the paragraph above the table explains the fifteen-minute catch-up. `additionalProperties: false` meant the two new fields had to be declared, and `is_primary`'s description — *where every unattributed click went* — was the schema asserting the behaviour being corrected. **Nothing asserted the breakdown's shape at all**, which is why this shipped; the new test writes both rollups directly, because producing the disagreement through the real jobs would mean racing their clocks. Sabotaged by restoring the old presentation: two assertions fail. |
| F68 | M36 | `demoReset` scopes three of its statements to one workspace and the rest to the organization, so an actor resolving anywhere else commits a half-reset that reports success — M36 removed the reachable path, not the asymmetry | `cmd/lctl/demo_data.go:63`, `:68`, `:73` — `link_tags`, `destinations` and `links` are deleted `WHERE workspace_id = $1`, while the rollups (`:87`), the click events (`:80`) and everything in `demoResetPhase2` are deleted by organization or by address | This is the defect that failed `make demo-update` at M36, diagnosed from the demo database: twenty-one catalogue links, their destinations and M34's four routing rules still present, and no click events, no rollups, no invitations, no seeded accounts and no second workspace. The reset had committed. M36 fixed it by pinning the actor (`demoActor`, D61), which closes the two ways the workspace could differ — the M25 switcher, and a run that dies between the seeder entering the second workspace and leaving it. The asymmetry itself remains, and one path to it survives: soft-deleting the workspace the demo lives in moves the pin to the next-oldest while the catalogue's rows stay behind, and `links` has no `deleted_at` cascade from `workspaces`. Not reproduced — no surface deletes a workspace that holds links | **Minor**, and only because the reachable path is now closed. The failure mode is the worst shape a seeder has: the destructive half commits, the rebuilding half refuses, and the operator is left with a demo that has lost its accounts and its history. The fix is to scope the three statements to the organization like everything else, and to keep the alias list as the thing that bounds them | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 6, minor — wrong behaviour nothing can provoke. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. Every statement in `demoReset` is scoped to the organization now, which is where the rollups, the click events and `demoResetPhase2` already were — the three that removed links, tags and destinations were the outliers. Uniformity rather than a widening: the demo has had two workspaces since M36 and every other statement had already reached this conclusion. **The existing seeder test could not catch it, and that is why it survived M36.** That test restores the owner's workspace before the reset runs, so the actor is always back in the catalogue's workspace and a workspace-scoped delete is indistinguishable from an organization-scoped one — narrowing the statement back leaves it green. The new test drives `demoReset` directly with an actor pointed at the second workspace: the state M36 stops the *seeder* producing and nothing stops a caller passing. Against the original scoping all 19 catalogue links survive the reset, which is the half-reset that commits and reports success. The comment in the seeder test that described the old scoping as current was corrected with it. |
| F73 | M40 | The verified-hostname cache has **no periodic reload**, so a replica that misses a pub/sub message serves a stale set until it restarts or hears another one | `internal/redirect/hosts.go` — `Reload` is called at boot, on `kindHost`, and by the subscriber's `flush` on every (re-)establishment; nothing is on a timer. The alias tier does not need one because every entry carries `REDIRECT_TTL`; this set has no TTL by construction, which is what makes it exact | Reproduced by reading. The gap is narrower than it looks: `Subscriber.establish` reloads on the first connection and on every reconnection, and `Subscriber.Run` bounds silence with `REDIS_SUBSCRIBER_READ_TIMEOUT` and probes rather than trusting it (F30's fix), so a replica that stops hearing gets a reload when it notices. What has no backstop is a **published message that is simply lost** while the subscription stays healthy — Redis pub/sub is at-most-once — and on an instance with no Redis at all, where a second replica would never reload | **Moderate** on any multi-replica deployment, restated 2026-08-04 (M45) from *"moderate … with Redis unavailable, minor otherwise"*, which named only one of the two limbs its own evidence establishes. Both limbs are moderate and neither needs Redis to be down: a published message simply **lost** while the subscription stays healthy — pub/sub is at-most-once, and `Subscriber.establish`'s reload only catches silence it notices — and an instance with no Redis at all, where the only reload sites are boot (`cmd/linkctrl/main.go:654`) and `HostCache.Refresh` (`internal/redirect/hosts.go:164-177`), which nothing but the subscriber calls — so a second replica never reloads at all. **Minor** on a single replica, where boot and on-demand reloads cover it. It fails in the direction of continuing to serve a hostname that has been unverified, which is exactly the staleness M40 exists to bound. A ticker calling `Reload` at `DOMAIN_VERIFY_INTERVAL` would close both for a query against a table of tens of rows. Out of spec here because m40.md asks for invalidation *wired into M23's pub/sub*, which is what shipped | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 1, moderate and above. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. A `host-reload` job re-reads each replica's verified-hostname set on `DOMAIN_VERIFY_INTERVAL`. **It takes no leadership, and that is the fix rather than an oversight** — the set is in-process, so a reload the leader performs does nothing for the other replicas; it is the only job on that clock without leadership and the comment says why. Both limbs close together: a message lost on a healthy subscription is never noticed because `establish` only catches silence the replica *notices*, and an instance with no Redis has no subscriber at all. `Reload` rather than `Refresh`, because a timer has no burst to collapse and inline means a slow query delays the next tick instead of overlapping it; a failure is logged and the replica keeps the set it has. **`cmd/linkctrl` had no tests at all**, which is part of why this shipped — the claim is about a job running without leadership, and `test/integration` cannot import package main. `jobs_test.go` is the first, and `make test-integration` and the Taskfile mirror now cover the package. Sabotaged by removing the reload. Four operator-facing claims corrected: `operations.md` gains the job row (and its `mail` row stops asserting F52's false half), `configuration.md` says the interval drives both halves and what `0` now costs, and `SECURITY.md`'s *on every replica* names the two mechanisms carrying it instead of the weaker one alone. |
| F106 | M44.9 | `DeleteOrganization` says the audit trail is all that survives; three rollup tables have no deleter anywhere | `internal/team/organization.go:211-212` and `:228-229`; `internal/store/query/organizations.sql:101-107`; against `internal/store/migrations/00400_analytics.sql:87`, `:102`, `:116` | `link_click_daily`, `link_dimension_daily` and `workspace_click_daily` carry `workspace_id` with no foreign key and no delete statement in any query file, so they outlive the tenancy. `click_events` and `visitors` are reclaimed by partition drop at `ANALYTICS_RETENTION_DAYS` (395 by default, `0` meaning never). The dispute half of this finding was **refuted**: the instance-wide open-dispute slot *is* reclaimable, because `decide` loads and writes by id with no organization predicate, so any holder of `destinations.review` can free the host, and the absent foreign keys are a recorded choice at `01600:49-52` | Minor, correctness-of-claim. The rows are unreachable by any reader, since every consumer scopes to a live `workspace_id` — so this is stale aggregate data and two inaccurate sentences rather than a disclosure. Fix note: adding foreign keys puts a constraint on the partitioned write path that `00400` and `00600:134` deliberately keep clean; delete the three rollups inside the existing transaction and correct the two comments | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 6, minor — wrong behaviour nothing can provoke. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. `DeleteOrganizationRollups` takes `link_click_daily`, `link_dimension_daily` and `workspace_click_daily` for the organization's workspaces, in the same transaction and **before** the organization row goes — the workspaces are the only way to name these rows and the cascade takes the workspaces. Not a security fix and the row is closed on its own terms: every reader scopes to a live workspace, so the rows were unreachable rather than exposed. What was wrong was the sentence. The dispute half stays **refuted** and nothing was built for it. Three claims corrected: `DeleteOrganization`'s *the audit trail, and nothing else*, which described a second survivor two sentences later and is now an enumeration; the same doc gains what no longer survives; and `docs/usage.md`'s *Nothing else is kept*. The test that should have caught this — `TestDeletingAnOrganizationTakesItsTenancyAndNothingElse` — now captures the workspace ids **before** the delete, because counting through a join afterwards finds nothing whether or not the rows remain, which is the assertion shape that would have passed against the defect. Sabotaged by removing the delete: three tables fail. |
| F67 | M36 | The per-link rule ceiling and the one-kind-per-split rule are both **read outside the transaction that writes**, so two concurrent creates can pass a check neither would pass alone | `internal/link/routing.go:144` — `CountRoutingRules` is called before `s.pool.Begin` in `CreateRule` (M34); `internal/link/split.go` — `CreateVariant` reads `CountRoutingRules` and `loadSplit` the same way, before its own `Begin` | Noticed while writing `CreateVariant`, which replicates M34's pattern deliberately so that the two writers behave identically. The window is real: two `POST /links/{id}/rules` at 20 rules each see 19, both insert, and the link ends with 21 — past the ceiling that exists because the whole list travels inside the cached snapshot and is walked on every uncached resolve. The split version is worse in kind rather than in size: two concurrent `POST /links/{id}/split`, one `weighted` and one `sequential`, both read a link with no arms, `ValidateSplitKind` passes for both, and the link ends with a **mixed split** — the exact state the validation exists to make impossible. The redirect path survives it (`Snapshot.Split` takes the first arm's kind and ignores the other), but the state should not be reachable. No `SELECT … FOR UPDATE`, no advisory lock and no unique constraint stands between them | **Minor.** Needs two writes to the same link inside a few milliseconds, which is a person double-submitting a form or a script, not ordinary use; and the worst outcome is a slightly over-long rule list or a split whose second kind is silently ignored rather than anything a visitor can observe as wrong. The fix is to move both reads inside the transaction — a `FOR UPDATE` on the link row would serialise every writer for that link — and it belongs to both milestones rather than to one, which is why it is a row rather than an in-spec fix here | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 6, minor — wrong behaviour nothing can provoke. Deliberately **not merged** with [F108](#closed) or [F127](#closed) by M45's queue hygiene, 2026-08-04. One class — a read-check-write with nothing serializing it — at three sites, but three files, three invariants, three fixes and three tiers: this row needs `FOR UPDATE` on the link row, F108 needs a transaction and a cycle predicate on the folder tree, F127 needs a row lock on an organization count and loses nothing when it races. Only F127 is approved as work. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. `LockLink` takes `FOR UPDATE` on the link row inside the transaction that writes, and every guard the write depends on is decided after it: the rule ceiling in `CreateRule`, and the ceiling plus the one-kind and one-fallback rules in `CreateVariant` via a new `loadSplitWith` that reads through the transaction's handle. The link rather than the rules, because Postgres takes `FOR KEY SHARE` on a parent when a referencing row is inserted — so a locked link cannot acquire a rule or an arm, including the first one, where a lock over the rules would have had no rows to take. The outer checks stay, and are now the *message* rather than the rule: they let a caller learn about a full link beside a bad destination in one answer. Each ceiling message is one function, so the two call sites cannot drift. **Two test designs passed against the defect before one held** — racing goroutines (the window is tens of microseconds; six racers produced one winner with the fix removed) and merely holding the link row (the insert's own `FOR KEY SHARE` blocks either way). What discriminates is moving the count *under* a create already parked on the lock; removing only the re-read then writes the twenty-first rule and fails three assertions. **Coverage is honest about its edge**: the deterministic test drives `CreateRule`, and `CreateVariant` shares the mechanism rather than having a race test of its own. |
| F51 | M32.9 | `invite.Redeem` verifies a password with no per-account lockout and no failed-login record | `internal/invite/invite.go:588-601`, against `internal/auth/service.go:299-327` | The headline is a conflation and dies: the per-IP login limiter **does** cover redemption — `router.go:204` wraps it in the *same* `"login"` bucket as `/auth/login`, deliberately, so alternating cannot double the budget. Verified live: 8 wrong guesses returned 404 with `failed_login_count=0` and `locked_until=NULL`; a burst of 20 returned 429 from the sixth. What survives is narrow: redeem lacks the per-**account** lockout that login also carries, so a *distributed* attacker holding a live, address-bound, single-use 32-byte token can guess that one account's password at per-IP-limited speed without tripping the cross-source lock. D27 weighs enumeration, not brute force, so this is genuinely unweighed | **Low.** Heavily gated by possession of a scarce token, and a successful guess yields nothing beyond what the same guess at `/auth/login` yields. Worth parity — record failures and honour the lock — not urgency | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 7, low. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. `Redeem` refuses a locked account and feeds the same counter, so the lockout an operator configured now covers both places a password is checked rather than one. The policy is built once in `main.go` and handed to both services, because two copies of a threshold are two things that can drift. `RecordFailedLogin` runs on `s.q` and not on the transaction's `q` — the refusal path returns an error and the deferred rollback would have discarded the count into nothing, which is the detail that makes this a fix rather than the appearance of one. The refusal stays `ErrNotRedeemable`, so D27's uniform answer is untouched and the lockout is not observable through redemption; a refusal does not spend the invitation. Both halves sabotaged separately — short-circuiting the lock check fails four assertions, forcing the threshold to zero fails the counter one — because a test claiming two things needs to fail for each of them. |
| F52 | M32.9 | Clearing `SMTP_HOST` on an instance that had one strands every queued message forever: the drain and the purge are gated on the same nil mailer | `cmd/linkctrl/jobs.go:189-191` and `:370-376` | Both guards confirmed. The package comment at `internal/mail/mail.go:9-11` — *"an instance with no `SMTP_HOST` … the outbox stays empty"* — is the actual defect: true only if SMTP was never configured, false across a configured-then-removed transition. The finder's *stronger half* is **not** an independent finding: `PurgeFinishedMail`'s `status <> 'pending'` is correct by design, because with a mailer no row stays pending — `Drain` claims and leases each one and transitions it to `sent` or, after 5 attempts, `failed`, and the lease recovers crash-interrupted rows. So the whole finding reduces to the SMTP-removal transition | **Low.** The relay is gone, so nothing deliverable is lost; the result is orphaned invisible rows (`Service.Pending` has no non-test caller) bounded by what was enqueued before removal, produced only by a deliberate uncommon operator action. Best fixed as an invariant correction or a one-time drain-then-purge on teardown | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 7, low. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. `AbandonUnsendableMail` fails pending rows past the 30-day window on the no-mailer path, and the purge job runs it in the `else` the mailer guard never had. Failed rather than deleted, so the row leaves by the same retention path as every other finished message instead of by a second delete, and its body goes for `MarkMailFailed`'s reason. Past the window rather than at once, so an operator who clears `SMTP_HOST` by mistake and restores it the same afternoon still gets their queue delivered. The finder's *stronger half* stays refuted and nothing was built for it. Two comments that asserted the false half — `internal/mail/mail.go`'s *the outbox stays empty* and `jobs.go`'s *with no mailer nothing is ever enqueued* — now say what is true across the configured-then-removed transition, and `docs/configuration.md` tells an operator what turning the relay off does to what is already queued. Sabotaged by inverting the window; it abandons both rows and fails. |
| F11 | M28 | `TestInvalidationReachesAnotherReplica` is flaky | `test/integration/invalidation_test.go:276` — "the second replica did not cache the alias in process" | Observed 2026-08-01 while running M28's gates. Two consecutive uncached full runs (`GOFLAGS=-count=1 make test-integration`) of the same tree: the first failed on this assertion, the second passed, and three earlier full runs of the same tree during the milestone passed. Nothing in M28 touches the redirect path, the resolver, Redis or the invalidation subscriber — the milestone's diff is `internal/team`, `internal/httpx`, `internal/ui`, `internal/auth`'s provisioning helper and one migration — so the change under test is not a plausible cause. The assertion is that a *second* replica has populated its in-process tier, which is reached by a prior request whose caching is asynchronous relative to the assertion; that is the shape a timing-dependent assertion has. | Minor, and test-only — no product behaviour is implicated. The cost is a gate that fails for the wrong reason, which is expensive in a loop that treats a failing gate as a stop condition: a flake here reads identically to a real regression in whatever milestone happens to be in flight, and the next person will spend the same run re-running it to find out which. | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 5, minor — one-edit corrections. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. The test warms the second replica's cache without waiting for the subscriber it started in a goroutine, and every establishment — including the first — flushes both in-process tiers, which is D20 and is correct. So the warm-up was racing a flush it had no way to see, which is why one run in five failed on a tree that never touched the redirect path. It now waits on `PUBSUB NUMSUB` for the channel before warming: Redis is asked, because there is no readiness hook and a test-only row is not where one gets added to the product. The barrier also makes the invalidation half prove itself — without it the publish could beat the subscription, the message be missed, and the establishment flush drop the entry anyway, so the test passed while demonstrating nothing about the thing it exists for. Eight consecutive runs green; sabotaged by publishing to a dead channel, which times it out. |
| F63 | M28 | `CreateWorkspace` gates on the identity's `workspace.write`, so a **workspace-scoped** admin adds workspaces to the whole organization — the one member-adjacent write M28's reopening left on the identity's union rather than on the membership that carried the permission (D44) | `internal/team/workspace.go:88` — `if !actor.Can(PermWorkspaceWrite)`, against `RenameWorkspace` and `DeleteWorkspace` immediately below it, which both resolve the permission against the *target* workspace through `writableWorkspace` | Read against the tree while building D44's bound, and the function's own doc comment states the mismatch: *"the authority being exercised is over the organization the caller is currently in"* — which is exactly the authority a workspace-scoped membership does not carry. `internal/store/query/members.sql` says a workspace-scoped membership grants authority over one workspace, and `00700_seed.sql` grants `workspace.write` to `owner` and `admin`, so any workspace-scoped admin reaches it. Not reproduced end to end: the D44 tests added in M28 cover the membership writes and stop where this begins | **Low.** It creates rather than reads or destroys, the new workspace is one the actor holds no membership in and therefore cannot act in, and no existing data is reachable through it — the cost is organization-wide clutter an owner must clean up, not access. Worth closing for consistency: after D44 it is the one write in the package that answers the old question | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 7, low. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. `CreateWorkspace` takes organization-wide `workspace.write` through `LoadMembershipAuthority(...).In(nil)` — D44's shape, and the same one `invite.orgWideAuthority` uses — instead of the identity's union. `Can` is still checked first, so somebody holding the permission nowhere gets the plain refusal rather than the scope one. The absence of a target workspace is what made the old gate look right; what settles it is that the *object created* belongs to the organization. The test refuses from an identity that legitimately holds `workspace.write` in its own workspace, because one holding nothing would have passed against the old gate and proved nothing; sabotaged by short-circuiting the new check, which fails it on both the error and the row count. `docs/SECURITY.md`'s enumeration of what only an organization-wide membership reaches gained *a new workspace* — the claim was already there and this row is what had made it false. |
| F25 | M32.9 | `ResolveWorkspaceForUser` does not check the organization's `deleted_at` while `ListWorkspacesForUser` does | `internal/store/query/auth.sql:26`–`:33` filters `w.deleted_at IS NULL` only; `internal/store/query/workspaces.sql:19`–`:25` joins `organizations` and filters both `w.deleted_at` and `o.deleted_at` | Verified by reading both queries. A workspace under a soft-deleted organization could be resolved into but never listed, so the switcher would mark nothing selected and a browser would show the first entry while the session acted somewhere else. **Latent today**: `DeleteOrganization` (`organizations.sql:71`) is a hard `DELETE` and nothing in the tree ever sets `organizations.deleted_at`, so the two queries agree in practice. It becomes live the moment anything soft-deletes an organization | Minor, latent. Filed because the asymmetry is invisible from either query alone | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 6, minor — wrong behaviour nothing can provoke. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. `ResolveWorkspaceForUser` joins `organizations` and filters `o.deleted_at IS NULL`, which is what `ListWorkspacesForUser` has always done — so the two statements of one scoping rule now agree on purpose rather than by accident. Fixed while latent and deliberately so: `DeleteOrganization` is a hard `DELETE` and nothing in the tree writes `organizations.deleted_at`, so nothing could provoke it, and the asymmetry is invisible from either query alone — which is why it survived to be found by review. `TestASoftDeletedOrganizationIsNeitherListedNorResolvedInto` sets the column directly, pins the resolve *and* the list halves beside each other, and was sabotaged: dropping the filter fails it on both assertions. |
| F40 | M32.9 | M24.5's theme-token guard scans one embed, and the rule it enforces reaches further: it cannot see `funcs.go` — the one file `input.css` explicitly names as a class source — nor the six hand-written redirect-tree pages | `internal/ui/theme_test.go:37` (`fs.WalkDir(files, "templates", …)`) against `internal/ui/static/css/input.css:13` (`@source "../../funcs.go";`) and the embed at `internal/ui/ui.go:35`; and against `internal/httpx/static/404.html`, `410.html`, `403.html`, `403-signature.html`, `password.html`, `password_wrong.html`, which sit in another package entirely | Demonstrated rather than argued. Rebuilt with the repo's own pinned `bin/tailwindcss` v4.1.14 — output byte-identical to the committed `app.css` (22760 bytes), so the harness is sound. Sabotaged `funcs.go`'s status-badge map to `bg-emerald-50 text-emerald-700 ring-emerald-200`: **both theme tests PASS** while Tailwind emits `.bg-emerald-50{`, `.text-emerald-700{`, `.ring-emerald-200{`, and `statusBadge` renders at four template sites, so the sabotage reaches a browser. Removing `static/vendor/` shrinks `app.css` by 798 bytes and drops six utilities that appear nowhere else, so auto-detection is scanning the vendored bundles too. **Folded in from F121 (M44.9), 2026-08-04:** the same scan line is also the reason six redirect-tree pages carry a second, hand-written palette nothing checks. That limb's live defect does **not** survive verification, and it is recorded here rather than dropped: m24.5.md scopes the override to *"every **dashboard** page"* and already exempts a non-dashboard surface, these pages are embedded byte slices with no template, and since M40 the redirect tree can answer on a hostname the dashboard's theme cookie never reaches. They do honour `prefers-color-scheme`, which is more than the milestone promised them. | **Minor**, upper end. No live defect — `funcs.go` currently uses tokens only. But m24.5.md stakes the milestone on this test: *"That test, not review vigilance, is what holds every later milestone's UI to both themes"*, naming M31, M37, M38, M41, M43 and M44. The design identified `funcs.go` as the special case and then built the enforcement so it cannot see it. The folded F121 limb is **Cosmetic** and stays so: an inherited-rule breach with no behaviour it contradicts, kept in this row because it is the same blind spot in the same scan and any widening has to decide about both roots at once. | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 5, minor — one-edit corrections. **[F121](#closed) (M44.9) was merged into this row** by M45's queue hygiene, 2026-08-04 — one guard, one scan root, and F121 already called itself *"the same blind spot in a different subtree"*. **[F130](#closed) was deliberately not merged** despite being filed as this row's sibling: it is a different guard in a different package (`internal/httpx/web_shell_test.go`) failing a different way — branches with zero inputs, not a scan with a blind spot — and no edit closes both. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. The theme scan reads `funcs.go` as well as the template embed — the one file `input.css` names explicitly as a class source, and the one file the guard could not see. Verified by performing the row's own sabotage: rewriting the status-badge map to raw emerald shades now fails by line and utility, where before it left both theme tests green while Tailwind emitted the classes and four template sites rendered them. Listed rather than walked, so the scanned set and the stylesheet's `@source` lines stay the same set. **The [F121](#closed) limb is closed by refutation, not by code**: the six hand-written redirect-tree pages stay unscanned, because m24.5.md scopes the override to *every **dashboard** page* and already exempts a non-dashboard surface, these are embedded byte slices with no template, and since M40 that tree answers on hostnames the theme cookie never reaches. Recorded in m24.5.md's reopening rather than dropped. |
| F46 | M32.9 | In the dark theme, white text on the two `*-hover` surface tokens fails WCAG AA — `accent-hover` 4.47:1 and `danger-hover` 3.67:1 — and neither pair is in the recorded contrast figures for either theme | `internal/ui/static/css/input.css:179` and `:193`, used with `--t-on-accent` on 21 buttons | Luminance recomputed independently. The resting states **pass** in dark (`accent` 6.29:1, `danger` 4.70:1) and are recorded at `input.css:93` and `:97`, so this is not the headline claim failing — it is two pairs that exist only in a transient state and were never measured. Both misses clear AA-large 3:1, and hover is unreachable by keyboard. WCAG 2.1 SC 1.4.3 has no hover exemption, and D21 restates the bar without a state carve-out | **Minor**, low end. Falsifies m24.5.md's *"Every token pair meets WCAG AA contrast (4.5:1 for body text) in both themes, recorded next to the token definitions"* — an unqualified *every* that two pairs escape. `#f43f5e` is rose-500, exactly what the milestone's own risk section predicted would drift on dark surfaces | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 5, minor — one-edit corrections. **Reopens [M24.5](phase-details/m24.5.md)** rather than landing in M45: it falsifies that milestone's own *"Every token pair meets WCAG AA contrast … in both themes"*. | **[M24.5](phase-details/m24.5.md) reopened**, 2026-08-05 — not M45 work, because it is that milestone's own claim that was false and workflow.md's reopening rule keeps the correction under the number that made it. Both now **darken** rather than lighten: `#4338ca` (7.90:1) and `#be123c` (6.29:1), recorded beside the token definitions. Darkening is the direction the light theme already takes, and lightening is the instinct that produced the defect — on a dark background it makes the button more prominent against the surface and less legible for the text on it, and only one of those is a WCAG requirement. `#f43f5e` is rose-500, which m24.5.md's own *Risks* section predicted would drift. |
| F102 | M44.9 | For the three Redis-shared limiters, the tracked-keys gauge is pinned at zero while the feature works, and the runbook's alert for a degraded shared limit has no expression that can fire | `internal/ratelimit/ratelimit.go:213-218` returns before the local shard write and before the overflow counter; `internal/observability/collectors.go:153-159` declares only `Len()` and `Overflows()`; `internal/ratelimit/shared.go:221` (`Fallbacks`) is referenced only by tests; `docs/operations.md:116` | On a healthy Redis the local table for `login`, `api` and `link_password` is never written, so `linkctrl_rate_limit_tracked_keys{limit="login"}` reads 0 — indistinguishable from no traffic — and the gauge's documented meaning at `collectors.go:154` is false. `operations.md:116` prescribes watching a rate that stays *"unchanged"*, which is not an expression. Narrowed by verification: a fallback **does** write the local table, so a non-zero reading is a working, undocumented fallback detector, and the runbook row does also point at a log line | Minor to moderate, observability only; fail-open is the accepted posture. The cost is that an operator following the shipped runbook cannot tell a shared limit from a fallen-back one, on the two limiters whose entire justification for a Redis round trip is that per-replica multiplication is unacceptable. Fix note: writing the local bucket on shared success is explicitly rejected at `ratelimit.go:208-212`, because a permanently empty bucket destroys failover; and `Fallbacks()` is monotonic, so a naive gauge alert latches forever after one transient blip | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 2, minor to moderate. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. A new counter, `linkctrl_rate_limit_fallback_total`, and a runbook row that is an expression. The tracked-keys gauge could not answer this and the row leaves it alone: a healthy shared limiter never writes its local table, so `Len()` reads zero and is indistinguishable from no traffic. The two fixes the row's own note rules out are both avoided — writing the local bucket on shared success is rejected at `ratelimit.go` because a permanently empty bucket destroys failover, and a threshold on `Fallbacks()` latches forever because it is monotonic, which is why this is a **counter** the alert reads as a rate. `docs/operations.md` said to watch a rate that *"stays unchanged"*, which is a description rather than an expression; it now carries one. |
| F109 | M44.9 | A recipient's email address reaches the process log unredacted, on the first bounce | `internal/mail/mail.go:181` appends `sendErr` to the returned error on **every** attempt, which `cmd/linkctrl/jobs.go:260` logs at ERROR as `slog.Any("error", err)`; also `mail.go:189-193`. `internal/mail/smtp.go:113` wraps the relay's response line, which routinely echoes the address | `internal/observability/logging.go:59-63` redacts by attribute **name** and `error` is not in the list, so `550 5.1.1 <alice@example.com>: User unknown` passes through. No `slog` attribute anywhere else in the tree carries an address, so this defeats a convention the package otherwise keeps — and `internal/webhook/client.go:288-296` unwraps `*url.Error` for exactly this reason one package over. Reachability is higher than first reported: it needs one bounce on the first attempt, not exhaustion of the retry budget. `mail_outbox.last_error` is **not** part of this — `recipient` is already a column on that row | Minor, and it needs SMTP configured. It moves an address from the database, which is access-controlled and retention-bounded, into the log stream, which is typically shipped elsewhere with no LinkCtrl-side bound. Same mechanism as F34 in a second package | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 6, minor — wrong behaviour nothing can provoke. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. `scrubAddress` replaces the recipient wherever a relay echoed it, before the error reaches `slog`. Replaced rather than dropped: the status code and the relay's wording are the operational content, and an error reduced to *delivery failed* trades a disclosure for an outage nobody can diagnose. Case-insensitive because SMTP is, and the local part alone is covered too, since a relay may answer *mailbox alice does not exist*. **`mail_outbox.last_error` is deliberately untouched** — that row already carries `recipient` as a column and is access-controlled and retention-bounded, which is exactly the distinction the row draws. Reachability was higher than first reported: one bounce on the first attempt, not exhaustion of the retry budget. |
| F59 | M32.9 | Two narrow privacy truncation gaps: `/48` preserves the entire client IPv4 for 6to4 addresses, and the in-memory salt cache retains salts past the database deletion described as the de-identification step | `internal/auth/session.go:167-186`; `internal/analytics/salt.go:86-96` and `:138-153` | Arithmetic reproduced: `2002:cb00:712a::1` → `2002:cb00:712a::/48`, whose bytes 2–5 are `203.0.113.42`. Every other v4-in-v6 scheme was tested and is safe — Teredo, NAT64 and ISATAP all embed in the low bits that `/48` discards, and `::ffff:` is folded explicitly with a comment naming the bug class. 6to4 is a single blind spot against a population RFC 7526 deprecated in 2015. The salt half was corrected in the **worse** direction: `Purge` evicts to `today−2` in the same call that deleted `today−2`'s row, so the leader is affected too and it reproduces on the single-replica default; and the eviction inside `For` runs only on a cache miss, so a replica that stops receiving clicks never trims. But the category is over-read: `README.md:126` and `salt.go:135` are claims about what a **database** holds — an erasure request, a backup, a dump — and a heap copy in a live process is none of those | **Trivial**, both. Filed together because both are one-line corrections on the privacy boundary that cost nothing to take, and because the salt one is the kind of off-by-one that makes a proud sentence not quite true. Neither is at risk from any attacker who does not already have more | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 8, cosmetic and trivial. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. Both one-line corrections on the privacy boundary, and both checked rather than assumed. **6to4** folds to its embedded IPv4 before masking: `2002::/16` carries the client's address in bytes 2-5, inside the `/48` an IPv6 address is kept at, so `2002:cb00:712a::1` masked to `2002:cb00:712a::/48` — `203.0.113.42` in full. Every other v4-in-v6 scheme was tested and needs nothing: Teredo, NAT64 and ISATAP all embed in the low bits a `/48` discards, and each is now a test case, because a fold applied too widely would turn a real IPv6 client into somebody else's IPv4 address. **The salt cache's predicate is now the SQL's**, not an approximation: a salt for day D carries `purge_at = D + 2` and the statement deletes `purge_at < now()`, so D is gone the moment D+2 begins — while the cache evicted `d.Before(today-2)`, which keeps exactly that day. The process held the one salt the de-identification step had just deleted. Eviction also runs on a cache **hit** now, and Purge trims unconditionally: the other evictor runs under leadership, so on a follower a hit was the only eviction there was. |
| F118 | M44.9 | Teardown writes alias reservations that the same statement's cascade deletes, for links on an organization- or workspace-owned domain | `internal/store/migrations/00300_links.sql:174` keys `reserved_aliases` to `domain_id` with `ON DELETE CASCADE`; `domains.organization_id` (`00300:12`) and `domains.workspace_id` (`02500:29`) both cascade from their parents; against `internal/team/organization.go:305` and `internal/team/workspace.go:243` | The reservation inserted immediately before the delete is removed by the cascade of that delete. Severity corrected sharply downward by verification: F28's exposure is the **shared default domain**, whose `organization_id` is NULL and which is untouched — `organizations.sql:104-107` says so and both regression tests use it — so the path that matters is intact. For a custom hostname the domain row is destroyed too, and re-serving that alias would require re-registering the hostname *and* passing M40's TXT check, at which point whoever does that controls the name anyway | Cosmetic. Self-defeating bookkeeping rather than self-defeating security: wasted inserts on every teardown that owns a custom domain, and an overstated sentence at `organization.go:216-225`. Fix note: `RESTRICT` makes organization deletion fail outright, `SET NULL` is impossible because `domain_id` is half the primary key, and re-keying reservations by hostname would reserve aliases on a name whose next owner proved control of it | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 8, cosmetic and trivial. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. Recorded at `DeleteOrganization`, where the overstated sentence was. `reserved_aliases` is keyed to `domain_id` with `ON DELETE CASCADE`, so for a link on a workspace's own hostname the reservation this teardown inserts is removed by the cascade of the same statement — wasted inserts rather than a wrong outcome. **Nothing is done about it and the comment now carries every reason**: F28's exposure is the shared default domain, whose `organization_id` is NULL and which teardown does not touch, so the path that matters is intact; re-serving a custom hostname's alias needs re-registering the name and passing the TXT check, at which point whoever did that controls it anyway; and each repair is worse — `RESTRICT` makes deletion fail outright, `SET NULL` is impossible because `domain_id` is half the primary key, and re-keying by hostname would reserve aliases on a name whose next owner proved control of it. |
| F120 | M44.9 | Both `Roles` functions filter on the identity's union rank, so a control offers a role the service then refuses — on the rank axis to a browser, and on the credential axis to an API key | `internal/team/team.go:254` and `internal/invite/invite.go:485` both filter on `actor.RoleRank`, while the ceilings read `here.Rank` (`team.go:211`) and `orgWide.Rank` (`invite.go:302`); `invite.Roles` is rendered by `internal/httpx/web_invitations.go:55`, and D43's API-key cap sits at `invite.go:302` inside `Create`, which both `POST /invites` and `POST /api/v1/invitations` reach | An organization-wide viewer who also holds a workspace-scoped admin row has a union rank of the stronger membership, so `Roles` lists admin while `invite.Create` compares against the organization-wide rank and refuses. `invite.go:296-301` names the exact hazard it did not fix. Both doc comments claim the opposite — *"a control cannot offer something `resolveRole` will then refuse"*. **Folded in from F62 (M27), 2026-08-04**, which found the same absent mirror on the other axis and, being older, carried anchors that had moved (`invite.go:450` → `:485`, the cap `:293` → `:302`). `Roles` mirrors D28's rank ceiling and not D43's cap, so a caller authenticated with an **API key** is offered `owner` and `admin` and gets a `403` on submit. A key reaches that page because `signedIn` (`internal/httpx/router.go:286`) is `RequireWebAuth`+`RequireOrganization`, `RequireWebAuth` (`internal/httpx/web.go:290`) tests only that an identity exists, and `BearerAuth` is applied outside `Session` (`router.go:413`) so a bearer token attaches one. It was raised at M27's step 3.4 as the worker's stated reason for *not* filtering `Roles` — *"the role form is signedIn-only so no key can reach it"* — and that reason is false. Bounded, and the bound is why it was never in spec: both routes call `invite.Service.Create`, where the cap sits, so the only reachable outcome is a refusal at submit. | Cosmetic. Nothing is reachable that the service permits; both ceilings hold and D44 is not weakened. The cost is a dropdown that 403s and three comments asserting it cannot — `invite.go:473` claims *"a control cannot offer something `resolveRole` will then refuse"*, and `Roles`'s own doc points at **the ceiling check** as the thing it applies, which is exactly what this row falsifies. F62 was filed at **Trivial** on the strength of that claim and is folded in here rather than triaged against it. `web_keys.go:55` is the same shape done the other way — a UI filter mirroring `NonDelegableScopes` — which is what makes the absent mirror here worth one row rather than none | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 8, cosmetic and trivial. **[F62](#closed) (M27) was merged into this row** by M45's queue hygiene, 2026-08-04: two rows against the same two functions, one of them resting its severity on a premise the other falsifies. One filter change answers both axes. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. **The false sentence is corrected and the filter is deliberately not narrowed**, which is the opposite of what the row's title suggests and is what working it established. `invite.Roles` was already right — it reads the organization-wide rank, exactly as `Create`'s ceiling does, and its comment explains why — so that axis had closed. `team.Roles` is the one whose claim was false: it said *a control cannot offer something resolveRole will then refuse*, while `resolveRole` compares against the authority covering the **target**, and this one list is rendered for a page whose rows target different workspaces and whose grant form chooses its target on submit. Narrowing to the organization-wide rank is right for invitations and **wrong here**: an invitation admits somebody to the whole organization, one ceiling governs it; a role assignment is per membership, and the weakest authority would hide admin from the workspace-scoped admin who may genuinely grant it. No single list is exact for many targets, so the comment now says the service is where exactness lives. D43's cap stays applied, because it is absolute rather than per-target. Absorbs F62. |
| F123 | M44.9 | The shared limiter's circuit breaker has no half-open state, so every concurrent request retries when the cooldown lapses | `internal/ratelimit/shared.go:250-257` — `allow()` reads under the mutex, sets nothing, and marks no probe in flight; `openUntil` is re-armed only in `recordFailure` at `:267`, after the failed call returns | The comment at `:228-233` promises *"an outage costs the timeout a few times and then nothing until the cooldown lets **one** request through to check"*, and `:61-62` repeats it. Severity corrected down by verification: the cost is `REDIS_READ_TIMEOUT` (50ms by default) once every five-second cooldown, a one per cent duty cycle rather than a stall, and it only becomes the herd the finding describes if an operator raises that timeout | Cosmetic to minor at defaults, moderate at a retuned read timeout. The comment is what the next person would trust when deciding whether the breaker needs work. Fix note: a compare-and-swap half-open must re-arm `openUntil` when the probe is **dispatched**, not on its result, or the probe's own timeout window is an open door; and a failed probe must re-open immediately rather than waiting for the failure threshold again. **Tier restated 2026-08-04 (M45) and held.** The range already reflects the widening: verification is what corrected it down, and the tier is stated as a function of the one setting that moves it, `REDIS_READ_TIMEOUT`. Cosmetic at the shipped 50ms, where the cost is a one per cent duty cycle; moderate wherever an operator has raised it, which is a documented thing to do. | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 8, cosmetic and trivial. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. A real half-open, built the way the row's fix note required: `allow()` **re-arms `openUntil` when the probe is dispatched**, not when it answers, so exactly one caller per cooldown reaches Redis and the rest are refused without paying the timeout. Deferring the re-arm to the result would leave the probe's own timeout window open to the same herd, which is the trap the note names. A failed probe needs no special case — the breaker is already armed, so it stays shut for another cooldown without having to reach the failure threshold again — and a successful one clears it in `succeed()`. Shown red by restoring the read-without-write: twenty of twenty requests admitted. The tier the row carried was Cosmetic at the shipped 50ms and moderate at a retuned `REDIS_READ_TIMEOUT`, which is a documented thing to do; the comment that promised this behaviour is now true at both. |
| F22 | M25 | Switching workspace from a link detail page lands on a 404 | `internal/httpx/web.go:105` puts `shell.Path` into the switcher's `next`, so the POST redirects back to a link id that does not exist in the target workspace | Reproduced: from `/links/<id>` while acting in Marketing, switching to Sales 303s back to that same URL and renders the Not-found page. A links surface that visibly fails to follow a switch, where falling back to `/links` would have worked | Minor. One dead end, recoverable by navigating; no data is exposed or lost | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 6, minor — wrong behaviour nothing can provoke. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. `switchTarget` collapses a link's detail page to `/links` in the switcher's `next`, and leaves every other path alone. The path named an id belonging to the workspace being left, so following it rendered Not found — a control that visibly fails to follow a switch, on the one surface where the collection view would have worked. To `/links` rather than `/dashboard`, because the reader was looking at links and still wants links. The table test asserts the eight paths that must stay put alongside the two that must not, since a rule that trimmed more would throw away the page a reader chose. |
| F42 | M32.9 | `Dispute.Liftable` conflates "a blocklist row caused this" with "an allow can succeed", so the review queue draws an **Allow** button on exactly the disputes the code already knows will 409 | `internal/dispute/dispute.go:236` (`liftableRules` admits `operator_blocklist`), `:545` (`entryToLift` refuses `SourceEnv`), `:712`, and `internal/ui/templates/pages/disputes.html:82-89` | Reproduced: `SeedBlocklist(["envblocked.example"])`, judge, file, Allow → `conflict: "envblocked.example" comes from LINKCTRL_DESTINATION_BLOCKLIST …`, dispute still `open`. A source census after a boot seed on a fully migrated database is `map[env:2 shortener:19]` — **no `review` row exists or can exist**, because the only runtime insert hardcodes `'env'` and the seed hardcodes `'shortener'`. The template renders the Allow form on `{{if .Liftable}}` and renders the *"nothing to remove"* warning only when `not .Liftable`, so an env-sourced dispute gets the button and not the warning. The 409 itself is deliberate, tested (`dispute_test.go:487`) and documented — it is the button that is wrong. M30's tier table is **not** falsified: a shortener row lifts cleanly, verified. Related and folded in: `01500:45` says `'review'` is *"what M31's owner review **will** write"* and `created_by` is written by no statement in the tree, which is the mechanical reason nobody noticed | **Minor to moderate.** Contradicts `dispute.go:140-145` — *"The page draws its buttons from this rather than making the owner discover it by clicking"* — for the most likely dispute an operator-configured instance will see, and `webError` turns the 409 into an error page rather than in-context guidance. Same family as F21: a control that looks like it does something and does not | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 2, minor to moderate. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. `Liftable` now asks both questions: whether the *rule* is list-backed, and whether the **entry behind this refusal** is one a decision may delete. The queue's two queries carry the entry's source through a LEFT JOIN, and `canLiftEntry` is spelled once and consulted by the flag the page draws from — so the button and the refusal cannot disagree, which is what this was: `entryToLift` refused an `env`-sourced entry and `Liftable` never asked. A source census on a migrated database returns only `env` and `shortener`, so this was not an edge case but the most likely dispute on any operator-configured instance. Both paths that return a freshly written row resolve the source too — `File` with one lookup, `decide` from the row it already read **before** the decision, since an allow has just deleted the entry and reporting *not liftable* because the lift succeeded would be the opposite of informative. Shown red by restoring the rule-only flag. |
| F47 | M32.9 | For an account that belongs to nothing, the notification bell's **View all** link is a dead end — `/notifications` sits behind `RequireOrganization` and 303s straight back | `internal/ui/templates/partials/nav.html:224`, against `internal/httpx/router.go:344` + `:287` and `internal/httpx/web_team.go:469` | Rendered the orphan page rather than reasoning about it: the only href not gated on anything is `/notifications`. `layout.html:33` invokes the bell unconditionally while the identity menu beside it gates Account and Feeds on `HasOrganization`. The finder's premise that organization deletion is soft is **false** (`organizations.sql:71-83` is a hard delete that cascades) — the conclusion survives for a better reason: `notifications.workspace_id` is nullable and all four producers leave it nil, so the workspaces cascade never reaches a notification row, and the count/preview queries are user-scoped. The orphan is also the *likeliest* holder: deleting an organization needs the owner role, and owners are the recipient class for audit-growth, invite-accepted and dispute-filed. `TestTheHeaderOffersNothingToAnAccountThatBelongsToNothing` cuts the nav *before* the bell, so despite its name it cannot see this | **Minor.** No authorization consequence — `RequireOrganization` is explicitly *"an affordance and not the authorization boundary"*. One dead control in a state whose design premise is that a nav made of redirects reads as broken, and the test written to enforce that premise stops one `div` short | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 6, minor — wrong behaviour nothing can provoke. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. The bell is gated on `HasOrganization`, like the identity-menu entries beside it. Its **View all** was the only href in the header not gated on anything, and `/notifications` sits behind `RequireOrganization` and 303s straight back — offered to exactly the account most likely to hold unread notifications, since deleting an organization needs the owner role and owners are the recipient class for audit growth, invitations and disputes. The existing per-page header test now expects zero bells wherever the shell has no organization, which is the assertion `TestTheHeaderOffersNothingToAnAccountThatBelongsToNothing` could not make: it cuts the nav one `div` before the bell. |

The redemption page says the opposite for the same instance (`invite.html:41`). A closed instance with a relay is an intended, documented state (`docs/configuration.md:285`), and the enforcement lives in redemption, not issuance, so `Create` is fully reachable there. Folded in: `invites.html:14-15` renders *"No mail relay is configured on this instance"* whenever `Emailed` is false, which is also the case when the outbox insert fails on a perfectly healthy mail-configured instance — the page already carries `MailConfigured` and the else-branch does not use it | **Minor.** A third copy of the D7 question, adjacent to F19 which covers the page-vs-service pair. The service has always known it: `Redeem` reads the same field. The renderer refuses a template referencing a key nobody passed, which is what caught the new branch immediately, and the fixture now renders **both** sides — a template with an `if` exercised on one side has an untested half, and the untested half is the one that only appears on somebody else's instance. |
