| F39 | M32.9 | `TestNoPageDataStructShadowsTheShell` never inspects **embedded** fields, so the exact F20 defect can be reintroduced by an ordinary refactor while the guard reports green | `internal/httpx/web_shell_test.go:126` — `readStruct` routes every anonymous field into `s.embedded` and never into `fieldOrder`, which the clash loop at `:54` is the only thing that iterates | Reproduced in a fresh scratch copy with three shadow shapes — a shared mixin naming `Path`, a local type named `Workspaces`, and an embedded `*auth.Identity`. **All three compile** (the attack's hypothesis that Go's ambiguity rules would reject them is false: `shell` *names* `Identity`, so an embedded one sits at depth 0 and wins), and the guard **passes**. Control with the named-field F20 shape fails correctly, so the test is not vacuous. Rendering the three through `text/template` reproduces F20's failure modes: two 500 on a plain field access, and the third renders an **empty workspace switcher silently**. No current page struct triggers it, but `struct{ shell; someMixin }` is the ordinary refactor that would — factoring `Notice`/`FieldErrors`/`Path` out of the dozen page structs that repeat them | **Moderate.** Falsifies m28.md's reopening, which asked for *"a test that fails if any page data struct in `internal/httpx` redeclares a field the shell already provides — the mechanism, not a comment"*: under Go's spec an embedded field **is** a field declaration. The test's own comment says *"Two limits, stated rather than hidden"*; this is an unstated third, which makes the comment worse than silence. Fix is one line in `readStruct` | No | M28, 2026-08-02. Closed under [phase-loop.md](phase-loop.md)'s *deferred overlap* rule rather than by owner approval — it falsifies M28's own first reopening, which asked for *a test that fails if any page data struct in `internal/httpx` redeclares a field the shell already provides*, and under Go's spec an embedded field **is** a field declaration. The exception is named in the commit message. `readStruct` now records every embedded field under the name Go gives it — the type identifier with any `*` and package qualifier dropped, so `*auth.Identity` is a declaration of `Identity` — and the clash check additionally walks the fields promoted from anything embedded that is *not* on the path to `shell`, which is this row's mixin shape: it does not shadow the shell's field, it makes the selector ambiguous, and a template resolving an ambiguous selector fails at render the way F20 did. Structs on the path to `shell` are skipped, because each is checked in its own right by the same loop. The test passed before the change, so it was shown red first against all three shapes recorded here — `sabotageOne` (mixin naming `Path`), `sabotageTwo` (embedded local `Workspaces`), `sabotageThree` (embedded `*auth.Identity`) — and the sabotage was removed by deleting the file that carried it. The *Two limits* comment this row called worse than silence now says one limit (methods) and names embedded fields explicitly. |
| F30 | M32.9 | The invalidation subscriber never detects a Redis that holds the connection open and stops answering: it blocks in `ReceiveMessage` with no deadline, never reconnects, never flushes, and `establish`'s liveness ping reports healthy | `internal/redirect/invalidation.go:207` (`ps.ReceiveMessage(ctx)`) and `:238` (the `ps.Ping(ctx)` health check) | Two go-redis facts verified at the installed v9.21.0 rather than taken on trust: `ReceiveMessage` is a bare loop over `Receive` → `ReceiveTimeout(ctx, 0)` → `deadline()` returning `noDeadline`, so `ReadTimeout` never applies; and `PubSub.Ping` is **write-only** — it writes the command and never reads a reply, so a nil return proves only that bytes entered the socket buffer. Reproduced on a purpose-built harness (not the finder's): real Redis behind a proxy discarding bytes both ways while holding sockets open, with a separate publisher. **Redis reported delivering the invalidation to 1 subscriber and it was silently lost**; `ReceiveMessage` stayed blocked 40s; `establish`'s ping returned nil in 0ms. Control with the socket *cut* — the only shape the existing tests cover — errored in 0.1s and recovered correctly. Keepalive measured off a real fd: `KEEPIDLE=300s INTVL=15s CNT=9` → death at **7m15s**, but only when probes go unanswered; an application-level stall (wedged Redis, proxy, sidecar) is ACKed by the peer kernel and the block is genuinely unbounded, proved by scaling keepalive to ~4s and still blocking at 30s. Note for whoever fixes it: go-redis's `Channel()` API would **not** help — its health check uses the same write-only `Ping` | **Major.** Falsifies m23.md's *"A dropped subscriber connection reconnects and degrades to TTL in the meantime — never to silence mistaken for freshness. Tested"* and `invalidation.go:238-240`'s claim that a successful ping means the subscription is *"genuinely live rather than merely not-yet-failed"*. Stale window is the remaining entry TTL, up to `REDIRECT_TTL` (24h default), because `mem.get` short-circuits before Redis is consulted; Redis itself stays correct, so it self-heals at expiry. Multi-replica only — the editing replica clears locally first. The test proxy already has a `stalled(true)` mode; it is pointed only at the writer | Yes — owner approved 2026-08-01 on M32.9's triage; **reopens [M23](phase-details/m23.md)**. Not via go-redis's `Channel()`, whose health check uses the same write-only `Ping`; the fix is a bounded read. | M23, 2026-08-01. Closed by bounding the subscriber's read with a new `REDIS_SUBSCRIBER_READ_TIMEOUT` (30s) and treating an expired read as a question rather than an answer: the subscriber pings and then waits for the *reply*, which is the one thing a stalled connection cannot produce, and only then concludes that the subscription has stopped delivering. Unanswered, it logs, drops both in-process tiers rather than serving them as current, closes the connection go-redis deliberately keeps after a mere timeout, and resubscribes. This row's evidence is right on every point and is left as written, including its note that `Channel()` would not help — that was checked again at v9.21.0 before the fix was written. Two things it did not reach. The flush moved from the reconnect to the failure, because against a Redis that never returns "flush when we reconnect" is a flush that never happens; and the fix had to be shown *not* to answer the finding by emptying the cache on every quiet interval, which is what the new test's first half asserts. `TestAStalledSubscriberStopsTrustingWhatItCannotHear` (`test/integration/invalidation_test.go`) was run red against the shipped tree first, timing out on the stale entry it went on to drop. D42. |
| F20 | M28 | `/workspaces` and `/members` return 500 on a plain GET once an organization has more than one workspace | `internal/httpx/web_team.go:40` and `:197` declare `Workspaces []team.Workspace`, shadowing the shell's `Workspaces []auth.Workspace` (`internal/httpx/web.go:90`); `internal/ui/templates/partials/nav.html:30` reads `.OrganizationName`, which `team.Workspace` does not have | Reproduced live on the test instance and confirmed by the server's own log: `render failed … page=workspaces error="ui: render page workspaces: template: nav.html:30:61: executing \"workspace_switch\" at <.OrganizationName>: can't evaluate field OrganizationName in type team.Workspace"`, and an identical line for `page=members`. Creation itself succeeds — the POST 303s and the row is in Postgres; the page you land on is what fails. **The trigger is >1 workspace in the CURRENT organization, not >1 overall**: an adversarial verifier held a user with six workspaces across two orgs and got 200 while acting in the one-workspace org, 500 on switching back, because `team.Service.Workspaces` filters on `actor.OrgID` (`internal/team/workspace.go:60`). A regression test written from the looser wording would pass without the fix. Two further symptoms: the switcher is silently **absent** from both pages rather than merely broken, so the two pages most about workspaces are the two you cannot switch from; and `renderWorkspacesError` re-renders the same broken page, so a duplicate workspace name answers 500 instead of 422 and validation messages on that page are unreachable. Every other authenticated GET route was swept and is unaffected. `/members` additionally needs `members.write`, so a read-only member sees no error at all | **Major.** Two shipped dashboard pages are unusable for any organization that has done the thing M28 exists to enable. It falsifies M28's own claims that member management and workspace management exist in the UI | Yes — owner reopened M28 on 2026-08-01 to fix it before [M32.9](phase-details/m32.9.md) completes | M28, 2026-08-01. Closed by giving the page field a name of its own: `membersPageData` and `workspacesPageData` now declare `OrgWorkspaces []team.Workspace`, and the templates and the render fixture follow it. The type stayed as it was — the two lists are genuinely different sets, the organization's workspaces with their per-row rights against every workspace the person may act in across organizations — so the name was the thing that was wrong, not the shape. This row's evidence is right on every point and is left as written; its `:40` and `:197` have since moved. The mechanism the reopening asked for is `TestNoPageDataStructShadowsTheShell` (`internal/httpx/web_shell_test.go`), which parses the package and fails on any struct embedding `shell` that redeclares one of its fields, so the next field to try this never reaches a browser. Two integration tests hold the behaviour, both written with two workspaces **in one organization** because the looser wording passes against the broken code. |
| F10 | M27 | `docs/usage.md` lists the never-grantable scopes and leaves out `audit.read` | `docs/usage.md`, the API keys section — "`apikeys.read`, `apikeys.write` and `org.delete` are never grantable to a key" | Noticed while correcting the sentence beneath it, which claimed `members.*` gates no endpoint. `auth.NonDelegableScopes` in `internal/auth/apikey.go` holds four entries: `apikeys.read`, `apikeys.write`, `org.delete` and `audit.read`. The prose names three. `docs/SECURITY.md` and `api/openapi.yaml` both name all four, so this is one document out of step with the other two rather than an ambiguity about the rule. Predates M27 — the omission dates from [M21](phase-details/m21.md), which added `audit.read` to the map and to the other two documents. | Cosmetic, documentation only. Minting a key with `audit.read` is refused whatever the guide says, so a reader who tries it gets a correct answer from the product; the cost is a reader who plans around a scope they cannot have. | No | [M28](phase-details/m28.md), incidentally, at `1d7c582` — not scheduled work. The scope documentation was rewritten while M28 documented `orgs.create`, and `docs/usage.md:171` now names all four never-grantable scopes including `audit.read`. Recorded rather than left open: the defect is gone, and an open row asserting a live defect that no longer exists is the same lie in the other direction. It was never owner-approved, which is worth seeing — an out-of-spec fix rode along inside an in-spec rewrite. |
| F2 | M23 | A stalled Redis stretches a link edit to about nine seconds | `internal/redirect/resolver.go`, `deleteFromRedis` | Measured while building M23, against a TCP proxy that accepts the connection and then never answers — which is what a stalled Redis looks like, as opposed to a refused connection, which fails in 264ms. `InvalidateAlias` took **9.07s** with the publish removed, so the time is the delete path alone: it makes three attempts, each given `RedisTimeout` (50ms in the test), and go-redis does not honour that context when it has to establish a connection to a server that never speaks. The measurement is in the transcript for M23; the same shape is reproducible with `redisProxy.blackholed(true)` in `test/integration/invalidation_test.go`. Predates M23 — the retry loop is M7's — and M23's own publish was made fire-and-forget so it adds nothing to this. | Moderate. Not data loss: the edit completes, the local tier is cleared, and correctness is unaffected. But `HTTP_REQUEST_TIMEOUT` is 15s, so an edit made while Redis is stalled comes within a few seconds of timing out, and a Redis stall is exactly when an operator is most likely to be changing things. | Yes — owner approved 2026-07-31, scheduled as [M26.6](phase-details/m26.6.md). That milestone corrects this row's attribution: the cause is two retry layers multiplying (the outer 3-attempt loop times go-redis's unset `MaxRetries` default of 3, over a 1s dial budget) rather than a context go-redis fails to honour. 3 × 3 × 1s matches the 9.07s measured. | M26.6, 2026-07-31. Closed by bounding the whole retry loop with one budget (`REDIS_INVALIDATE_BUDGET`, 250ms, D26) rather than by lowering a timeout. This row's evidence is right and is left as written: go-redis really does ignore the context on a stalled read, and the *Reviewed* cell's correction of it — two retry layers multiplying over a 1s dial budget — is wrong on both counts. Measured for M26.6: `MaxRetries` does not multiply a read that timed out, and `REDIS_DIAL_TIMEOUT` is not involved at all against a server that accepts the connection. The arithmetic is 3 outer attempts x the client's `ReadTimeout`, and the 3s in that product was go-redis's own default in `redisProxy.client`, which sets no timeouts. The shipped client sets 50ms from `REDIS_READ_TIMEOUT`, so the same stall cost a deployment **215ms, not 9.07s** — the severity above was measured through a client no deployment runs, and the edit was never near `HTTP_REQUEST_TIMEOUT`. What was genuinely broken is that a documented, operator-tunable knob was multiplied by three on a path an operator waits on. |

The header renders the signed-in address as a plain `<span>` that does nothing, with sign-out as a sibling button and Account as a fifth navlink competing with Dashboard, Links, API keys and Notifications. Nothing is broken — every destination is reachable — so this is design, not defect. The convention it departs from is the one every dashboard the reader has used follows: the name is the control, and identity-scoped actions hang off it. Same shape as F4, which this repo recorded as a finding rather than a feature and folded into a milestone: the function exists and the placement is wrong. Note that a dropdown has to work without JavaScript to satisfy the `ui` stays stdlib-only rule — `<details>`/`<summary>` is the only mechanism available, and CSP forbids the inline handler the obvious implementation would reach for. | Minor. No claim is false and nothing is unreachable. The cost is a top-level nav slot spent on Account and a person hunting for sign-out, which is the same discoverability cost F4 carried. | Yes — owner approved 2026-07-31, scheduled as [M26.5](phase-details/m26.5.md) | M26.5, 2026-07-31. The address became a `<button popovertarget>` opening a panel holding Account and Sign out, and both left the top-level nav. This row's evidence is wrong about the mechanism and is left as written: `<details>`/`<summary>` was not "the only mechanism available" — the Popover API is also declarative, also needs no script, also needs no CSP waiver, and unlike a disclosure it closes on Escape. The finding is not reopened — the nav still holds only places work happens, which is what it asked for; the number it settled on was the part the walkthrough disagreed with. `TestTopLevelNavHoldsThreeDestinations` was renamed `TestTopLevelNavHoldsTwoDestinations` and updated, never deleted. |

| F5 | M26 | Two shipped documents still say the audit log has no behaviour | `docs/SECURITY.md`, the "What is not defended" list — "**The audit log has no behaviour.** The table is partitioned and maintained; nothing writes to it. Do not rely on it for forensics yet."; and `README.md`, the "Not built yet" list — "**No audit log behaviour**, no folders, no custom domains…" | Noticed while making the same two lists true for M26's mailer. SECURITY.md's line is the more consequential of the two: it explicitly tells a reader not to rely on the log for forensics, which is advice against a defence that now exists. | Minor, and documentation only — nothing in the product misbehaves. It makes M21's own claim false rather than M26's, so it reopens M21 rather than arriving as new scope — which is the owner's call to schedule, not this milestone's to take. | Yes — owner approved 2026-07-31 | Not by reopening M21. `m21.md` enumerates the docs that milestone promised to update — the `partitions.go` comment and operations.md — and it updated both; every bullet it carries is satisfied, so an `in progress (reopened)` row would assert incompleteness that is not there. | F3 | M24.5 | Dark mode has no effect on the UI: the light tokens are unlayered and beat every dark rule | `internal/ui/static/css/input.css` — light `:root` block at line 88, dark rules at lines 139–178 (`@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark)`) and 182 (`:root[data-theme="dark"]`) | Owner report, 2026-07-31, and confirmed by construction rather than inferred from the symptom. The light `:root` block sits at the top level of the file; both dark blocks sit inside `@layer base`. CSS cascade layers give **unlayered** normal declarations priority over layered ones regardless of specificity, so `:root { --t-surface: #f8fafc }` unconditionally beats `:root[data-theme="dark"] { --t-surface: #0f172a }`. Walking the built `app.css` top level confirms it survives the build: the dark override is inside the `@layer base { … }` construct, and `:root { color-scheme: light; … }` is emitted after `@layer utilities` closes, as its own unlayered top-level rule. Both dark paths are dead, the explicit override and the `prefers-color-scheme` one, which is why `color-scheme: dark` never takes either — matching "changing to Dark Mode does nothing". The server side is *correct* and is not implicated: the demo serves `data-theme="dark"`, `data-theme="light"` and no attribute across the three cookie states. **The milestone's own enforcement cannot catch this**: `TestTemplatesUseThemeTokensOnly` asserts templates name tokens, and the AA contrast figures are recorded in comments, so nothing tests that a token's *value* ever changes. A fix wants a test that fails on this specific cascade, not more contrast arithmetic. | **Critical.** [M24.5](phase-details/m24.5.md)'s headline claim — every dashboard page follows `prefers-color-scheme`, and an explicit override renders in the chosen theme — is false in the shipped build. The milestone was reopened rather than corrected by a successor, so the status row now reads `in progress (reopened)` and no longer asserts something untrue. The AA contrast claim is false by consequence: the dark figures describe values no browser applies. M24.5 was placed before M25, M31, M37, M38, M41, M43 and M44 precisely so those build inside a working theme; every UI milestone landing before this is fixed is written and reviewed against a dark theme that cannot be seen, which is the retrofit the ordering existed to avoid. | Yes — owner-reported; folded into reopened M24.5, 2026-07-31 | M24.5, 2026-07-31. Every `--t-*` block is now unlayered, so the dark selectors win on specificity and source order; `TestThemeTokensShareOneCascadeLayer` was shown failing against the stylesheet built from `b289a39` before it counted, and the live check now resolves the cascade against the composed stack rather than confirming the attribute. |
| F4 | M24.5 | The theme control sits in the page footer, where nobody looks for it | `internal/ui/templates/layout.html`, the `theme_switch` template near the footer | Owner report, 2026-07-31. It is a placement judgement the milestone did not make — appearance preferences are conventionally in account settings or the top-level chrome, and the footer is the one region of the page a person scanning for a setting will not read. Related to F3 only by file: the control works, it is merely hard to find, and moving it fixes nothing about F3. | Minor. No claim is false and nothing is broken. It is discoverability, and it costs a person one hunt through the page rather than any data or correctness. | Yes — owner-reported; folded into reopened M24.5, 2026-07-31 | M24.5, 2026-07-31. The control moved to `/account`, with the sign-in page rendering the same partial; `TestExactlyOneAppearanceControlPerPage` asserts the count on every page, so the layout's site cannot come back. |
| F76 | M44.9 | A domain verification can be made to verify a hostname the caller does not control — including the instance's **own** app or link host | `internal/link/verification.go:166` reads the row, `:175` resolves DNS (up to `DOMAIN_VERIFY_DNS_TIMEOUT`, 5s), `:201` writes `MarkDomainVerified(ctx, row.ID)`; `internal/store/query/domains.sql:162-170` is `WHERE id = $1 AND deleted_at IS NULL`. Same shape at `verification.go:301`/`:312` in `ReverifyDomains` | Read-check-write with an attacker-pausable network call in the middle, no transaction and no `FOR UPDATE` (`domainForWrite` is a bare `GetDomainByID`). `RenameDomain` is concurrently reachable at `PATCH /api/v1/domains/{domainID}` and clears `verified_at`; the late write then fills exactly that NULL through `COALESCE(verified_at, now())`. Nothing re-reads or re-compares the hostname, and `MarkDomainVerified`'s `RETURNING` row is consumed only to stamp the audit record at `:206-208` — **so the audit trail corroborates the hijack instead of catching it**. Adversarial verification established the impact is wider than a customer hostname: D69 and `hostname.go:39-96` refuse no self-host name, and `customHostRouter` sits in front of both router branches (`router.go:826`), so renaming the in-flight row to the instance's **app host** hides the dashboard and API behind the custom mux (ops + root + aliases only), and renaming it to the **link host** repoints every alias lookup at the attacker's `domain_id` via `redirect.go:177-179`. DNS and certificates for those names already exist | **Critical.** Falsifies m40.md's *"Only after `verified_at` is set does the host router resolve aliases on that hostname"* and `domains.sql:71-76`'s rename rationale. Reachable by any principal holding `domains.write` in any workspace — every organization owner and admin, and one registration on `LINKCTRL_SIGNUP_MODE=open`. The attacker owns the nameserver, so they choose the window length. Fix note: a transaction around read and write does **not** close it, because the rename commits in the gap between two separate transactions, and `FOR UPDATE` across the lookup holds a row lock for 5s inside a 500-row job. The shape that closes it is a conditional write (`WHERE id = $1 AND hostname = $2 AND verification_token = $3`) with a zero-rows-affected branch, applied at both sites | No | **[M40](phase-details/m40.md), reopened.** `MarkDomainVerified` is predicated on the hostname and the token the check was made against, with an explicit zero-rows branch, at both call sites; the audit record is stamped from the checked hostname. D92 records why a transaction and `FOR UPDATE` were both refused. `TestARenameCannotStealAVerificationInFlight` and `TestTheJobCannotVerifyANameItDidNotCheck` rename into the instance's own link and app hosts mid-lookup, and both fail on a build without the predicate. |
| F84 | M44.9 | Custom-domain re-verification can be starved indefinitely by one tenant, which silently disables the only mechanism that takes a lapsed or hijacked hostname out of service | `internal/link/domains.go:206-252` (`RegisterDomain`) has no per-workspace count check, unlike campaigns and folders; `internal/link/verification.go:292-302` walks one ordered batch serially; `internal/store/query/domains.sql:140-147` orders `verification_checked_at NULLS FIRST, id LIMIT $row_limit` (`DOMAIN_VERIFY_BATCH`, 500); `cmd/linkctrl/jobs.go:382` bounds the pass at ten minutes | Each row blocks up to `DOMAIN_VERIFY_DNS_TIMEOUT` (5s) against a nameserver the registrant controls, so 500 × 5s is 41 minutes against a 10-minute budget. Adversarial verification **corrected the mechanism**: rows the loop reaches do write `verification_checked_at`, so NULL rows drain — but `RenameDomain` resets that column to NULL (`domains.sql:82`), so one `PATCH` per row returns it to the head of `NULLS FIRST` indefinitely. Verified tenants carry non-NULL watermarks and therefore sort last, so `UnverifyDomain` (`verification.go:363`) never fires for them | **High**, and cross-tenant. What is starved is not throughput but the D70/D71 hard stop — the deleted-CNAME plan that m40.md calls *"the whole security story of this milestone"* — which stops running instance-wide while appearing healthy. Needs one workspace with `domains.write` and one zone delegated to a blackholing nameserver; the hostnames need not exist. Fix note: raising `DOMAIN_VERIFY_BATCH` makes it strictly worse, and adding lookup concurrency without a per-workspace domain cap turns the leader into an outbound DNS amplifier any tenant can aim | No | **[M40](phase-details/m40.md), reopened.** The pass is drawn in two classes and serving hostnames are checked first, inside the one configured batch — a rename un-verifies, so registration churn can crowd only the pending class. `RegisterDomain` caps a workspace at `domain.MaxDomainsPerWorkspace` (25). The batch was not raised and no lookup concurrency was added; nothing reaps a registration. `TestServingHostnamesAreCheckedBeforePendingOnes` and `TestAWorkspaceCannotRegisterUnboundedHostnames`. |
| F78 | M44.9 | An exhausted one-time or max-click link serves its destination to `HEAD`, repeatably and forever | `internal/httpx/redirect_gates.go:229-235` — `if !gated \|\| r.Method == http.MethodHead { return gatePassed }`, which skips the budget rather than merely not consuming it; `gatePassed` is `answered:false`, so `redirect.go:405` writes `Location` and 302 | **Reproduced live.** A `one_time` link answered 302 with the destination to GET #1, 410 to GET #2, and then 302 with `Location: https://example.com/secret-destination` to three successive `HEAD` requests. `record()` skips HEAD (`redirect.go:454`), so the disclosure leaves no click row. The budget is never *read*, only never *written* — and `gate.Service.Budget` (`internal/gate/gate.go:205`) is an existing non-consuming read that would let HEAD answer 410 without spending | **High.** Falsifies m35.md's *"exceeded serves 410"*, which carries no method carve-out. It does **not** falsify `docs/SECURITY.md`'s *"HEAD never spends a click"*, which is satisfied — the recorded decision bought non-consumption and the code delivered non-checking, which is strictly more. Narrowed by verification: signature and password gates both run first and both refuse HEAD, so the disclosure needs a link whose only gate is the budget — which is the ordinary one-time link, the shape the feature exists for. Untested: no `MethodHead` case exists in the gate tests. Fix note: making HEAD consume destroys the feature and is the outcome D53 names as unacceptable; the fix is the non-consuming read plus a 410, and it must not become a per-request query for links that are not exhausted | No | **M35, reopened.** `budgetGate` sends HEAD to `budgetPeek`: the non-consuming `gate.Service.Budget` read the finding names, plus 410, on the **HEAD branch only** — a GET still performs exactly the one upsert it always did, with no read put in front of it. The predicate is `consumed >= limit`, the same comparison `ConsumeClickBudget` makes in its conflict clause, so a ceiling lowered after exhaustion still reads as spent where `exhausted_at` would not. D94. `TestHeadOnASpentLinkIsRefused` asks three times — one request cannot tell the fix from a HEAD that consumed, because consuming would also produce a 410 — and re-reads the counter afterwards; it fails on a build without the fix. |
| F79 | M44.9 | Signed links are verified against the instance's default domain rather than the link's, so they are wholly broken on custom domains and a signature crosses between same-alias links | `internal/httpx/redirect_gates.go:125` passes `h.DomainID`, documented at `internal/httpx/redirect.go:51-53` as *"resolved once at boot"*, while `internal/link/gates.go:132` signs with `row.DomainID`. `redirect.go:176-179` computes the request's real domain into a local that `passGates` (`:361`) never receives | `grep -n 'h\.DomainID' internal/httpx/` returns exactly two sites, both in the gates, and the field is never reassigned. `internal/gate/signature.go:169` mixes the domain id into the HMAC, so signer and verifier disagree whenever `links.domain_id` is not the boot default — which D71 makes the norm, since a workspace's verified hostname becomes the default for its new links. `redirect.Snapshot` carries `LinkID` and `WorkspaceID` and no domain, so no second path supplies it. No test covers a custom-domain gate: every gate fixture wires `DomainID` from `ResolveDefaultDomain` | **High.** Two failures from one omission. Signature verification on a custom domain fails closed (403) for the legitimate holder — a shipped feature that is 100% non-functional on another shipped feature. And a signature minted for alias `X` on the default domain verifies against a *different* link with alias `X` on a custom host in the same workspace, which is exactly the property the domain id is in the MAC to prevent. Falsifies `internal/gate/signature.go:42-45`, `docs/SECURITY.md:64` and the M35 decisions entry, which calls the case *"not hypothetical"* and names M40 as the milestone that creates it. The same boot constant keys the per-alias password bucket at `:211`, falsifying its comment that *"the same alias on two domains is two links"* — one defect, two consequences, and the second one fails safe. Both limbs, because the second is the same boot constant read in the same file under a comment that had stopped being true. D94. `TestASignedLinkWorksOnACustomHostname` and `TestASignatureDoesNotCrossHostnames` are the two consequences and both fail without the fix — 403 for the legitimate holder, and 302 carrying the *other* link's destination for the crossing case — and `TestThePasswordBucketIsKeyedOnTheRequestsHostname` reads which of two buckets a single guess emptied. **The fix note's expectation that this invalidates every minted signature is the wrong way round**: `link.Sign` already signed with the link's own `domain_id`, so default-domain signatures are unaffected and custom-domain ones, which never verified at all, begin working. D94 records why. |

M42's reopening deleted the multiplication: `webhook.DeliveryConcurrency = DrainBatch` (`internal/webhook/webhook.go:94`) and `Drain` dials the claimed batch together under a `WaitGroup` (`:254-268`), so a drain costs **one** `WEBHOOK_TIMEOUT` — at most 1m against a 5m bound (`cmd/linkctrl/jobs.go:368`) that belongs to `runWebhooks` alone and is shared with no other job. Each sub-fact was re-checked against the tree rather than assumed: (1) the attempt is still spent at claim time before any socket (`internal/store/query/webhooks.sql:105-107`), which is deliberate and is stated at `:87-94` as the thing that bounds a crash loop; (2) `MarkWebhookRetry` and `MarkWebhookAbandoned` still take the delivery context (`internal/webhook/webhook.go:313-317` and `:322-328`), so a cancelled context still leaves a row unmarked — but the only remaining way to cancel it is process shutdown, which costs one attempt per claimed row per shutdown rather than one every sixty seconds, and the sixty-second lease at `webhook.go:236-240` brings the row straight back; (3) `internal/config/config.go:972-978` still never validates `WEBHOOK_TIMEOUT` against the five-minute job bound — checked, because this row asked for it — and no longer needs to, since a 1m ceiling cannot reach a 5m bound. The coupling this row said was undeclared **is now declared**: `config.go:958-971` names `webhook.DeliveryConcurrency` and the 30s tick in prose, and says why it is prose rather than an import. The severity restatement M45 owed this row is discharged by the closure: the **Moderate** tier rested entirely on twenty sequential dials fitting inside a five-minute bound, and there are no longer twenty of them. [F82](#closed)'s closing note already recorded that this row was *"no longer reachable at any permitted `WEBHOOK_TIMEOUT`"* and left it open as out of scope; this is that observation carried through. |

The operator's two are the SMTP relay and the feed, unchanged. The two tests this row named as the cost were changed and say why: `internal/ui/feeds_test.go` grew from two fixtures to four and its *not configured* case now pins *"Nothing you point a link at leaves this instance"*, and `test/integration/feed_test.go:503` pins the same string — the same claim, in wording that is true. | F87 | M44.9 | A sequential split on a password-gated link advances the rotation twice per visit, so at any even arm count half the arms are served to nobody | `internal/httpx/redirect.go:327` runs `h.split` before `passGates` at `:360`; `internal/httpx/redirect_gates.go:153-156` answers any non-POST with the challenge; `internal/httpx/redirect_split.go:136` calls `Rotate` unconditionally | The challenge GET reaches `:327` and consumes a position, then is answered with the form; the verifying POST is not refused (`redirect.go:274-275` exempts `HasPassword`), reaches `:327` again and consumes a second. The arm served is therefore always index `(2k-1) mod N` — **for N=2 that is `arms[1]` on every visit, forever, and `arms[0]` is never served to anyone**. D57 states the opposite at `decisions.md:9408`, repeated verbatim at `Plan.md:810` and `redirect_split.go:117`: *"the distribution is unaffected — whether a gate passes is independent of which arm came up."* That is true of a refusal and false here, because the password gate is the one gate that manufactures a guaranteed second request. `test/integration/split_test.go:215-236` pairs sequential with a one-time gate and uses three arms, an odd count that would mask it | **Moderate.** A shipped A/B feature silently reports zero traffic to half its arms whenever the link is also password-gated, and `click_events.destination_id` faithfully records the arm that was served, so the analytics confirm the skew rather than expose it. Fix note: moving `h.split` after `passGates` is the obvious move and breaks M35's load-bearing ordering (`decisions.md:9224-9231`) — the budget gate would then run before the destination exists, so an unforwardable deep link on a one-time link would spend its single click on a request about to 404. The fix belongs in the challenge branch | Approved as work 2026-08-04 — [M45's triage](decisions.md), the moderate non-security set. Fixed under **M45's own number**: m45.md's Findings bullet is this work, so no milestone is reopened. | **M45** (`docs/build-notes/decisions.md`, *M45, four ways the redirect path was not the path it described*), and in the challenge branch exactly as the fix note required. `challengePending` — one comparison over the snapshot and the request method — is asked **before** `h.split`, so a request that is going to be answered with the password page chooses no arm and writes no rotation; the POST that answers it chooses the arm that is actually served. `h.split` did **not** move after `passGates`: the gates run after the destination is known so an unforwardable deep link is a 404 before the budget gate can spend a one-time link's only click, and reordering would have bought this at that price. The reasoning is now written down rather than assumed — the password challenge is not a refusal but the first half of a visit arriving in two parts, which is what makes it the one gate D57's *the distribution is unaffected* does not cover. That sentence is corrected in `Plan.md` at both sites and in `redirect_split.go`; decisions.md is append-only, so the new entry corrects `:9425` rather than editing it. A **wrong** password still spends a position, which is D57's case and stays true. Driven by `TestASequentialSplitBehindAPasswordAdvancesOncePerVisit` — **two** arms, the count that makes the failure total, and the rotation asserted between the challenge and the POST rather than only at the end — sabotage-verified. |

| F88 | M44.9 | On a single-host deployment, a `Host` spelling that does not fold serves a customer's verified hostname the dashboard, the API and the default domain's aliases — and on a split-host deployment the same spelling of a configured hostname gets the ops-only 404 | `internal/config/config.go:643-657` (`CanonicalHost`) folds case and strips only `:80`/`:443`, never a trailing dot; `internal/redirect/hosts.go:117` keys the verified-host cache through it and `:106` records that it does so deliberately; `internal/httpx/redirect_hosts.go:62` reaches that cache with the raw `r.Host`; `internal/httpx/router.go:867-871` builds the combined mux that is the fall-through arm on a single host; `internal/httpx/router.go:912-925` is the split-host `switch` | `Host: go.customer.example.` or `go.customer.example:8080` misses the host cache, falls through, and is served by `registerOps + registerApp + registerRedirect` — and `redirect.go:176-179` then resolves against `h.DomainID`, the instance default, so another workspace's aliases answer on the customer's name. Reachable over HTTPS: SNI carries no trailing dot per RFC 6066, so the certificate handshake completes and Go passes `r.Host` through unnormalized. The whole M40 suite pins `SplitHosts: true`, the one configuration where the fall-through is the ops mux. **Folded in from F72 (M40)**, which found the same gap from the other side: `CanonicalHost("lnk.links.test.")` returns itself, matching neither `appHost` nor `linkHost` in `hostRouter.ServeHTTP`, so on split hosts the request lands on the ops mux. The mismatch is entirely request-side — `domain.ValidateHostname` drops a trailing dot at `internal/domain/hostname.go:66` and the unique index is on `lower(hostname)`, so a stored name can never carry one — and commit `6f95079` (*"Fold the dot before any tier reads the host"*) folded it for **destinations** and not for the `Host` header. Pre-existing since the split-host router; M40 inherited it rather than introducing it | **Moderate.** F72 filed the same normalization gap at Minor on the strength of a conclusion this row refutes — *"it fails closed (a 404, never a wrong host's links)"* — which holds only for split-host; on single-host the failure is open, and serving the default domain's aliases is exactly "a wrong host's links". F72 also named only the trailing dot, not the port spelling. One `CanonicalHost` edit reaches the split-host router, the single-host mux and the verified-host cache together, which is why the two rows were never separable | Approved as work 2026-08-04 — [M45's triage](decisions.md), the moderate non-security set. **[F72](#closed) (M40) was merged into this row** by M45's queue hygiene, 2026-08-04: one function, one gap, and F72's stated conclusion was false, so it could not have been triaged on its own terms | **M45** (same entry), and the two limbs are closed by two functions because they were always two questions. `config.CanonicalHost` now folds the DNS root dot, in both the ported and unported branches — a lone `TrimSuffix` on the whole string misses `host.:8080`, which the test pins — and it still **keeps** a non-default port, because `SplitHosts()` compares two configured origins through it and stripping the port would collapse a deployment serving the dashboard and short links on one name and two ports into a single-host one. The verified-hostname cache asks the new `config.HostOnly`, which is `CanonicalHost` with any port dropped as well: `domains.hostname` is stored bare, validated bare, and served on whichever port the listener uses, so there is no port to compare against. `HostCache.Reload` keys through the same function, so the two sides cannot drift — which is the failure one level up. `TestHostOnlyIsWiderThanCanonicalHost` pins the direction: the narrow spelling is the one that fails silently. **F72's severity argument is refuted in the test rather than in prose**: `TestAHostSpellingThatDoesNotFoldStillReachesItsOwnHostname` runs on a **single-host** fixture built for it, and the sabotage answered `https://example.com/the-instances` on the customer's hostname and served `/links` — the open failure, not a 404. `TestAConfiguredHostnameFoldsItsTrailingDot` is F72's own case on split hosts. The collision widening could create is named in `hosts.go` and in decisions.md: it still needs a DNS TXT record under the operator's own name, so the precondition is unchanged and only the set of `Host` spellings is. |

The setting **propagates** rather than the setter being widened, which is what the fix note ruled out: a per-hostname policy under `domains.write` — which [F70](#closed) records as reaching every organization's owner and admin — would let a workspace switch off an enforcement the operator set, a wider hole than the one being closed, and per-domain settings are D69's parked question waiting on D38's missing instance-level principal. `SetDefaultDomainBotBlocking` became `SetBotBlockingForEveryDomain`, writing every undeleted row, and `CreateDomain` inherits the default's current answer so a hostname registered afterwards does not reopen the gap. **Both columns are written together in both places**, because `01800`'s CHECK refuses *enforced without blocking* per row and a single-column propagation would be refused on its way out. | F71 | M39 | `demoCoverage`'s idempotency assertion is **time-dependent**: today's clicks are generated at minute granularity and dropped when they land after the current instant, so two seeder runs seconds apart on either side of a minute boundary produce different click counts and the test fails | `cmd/lctl/demo_data.go:361` and `:387` — `if at.After(now) { continue }`, against `at` built from `rng.IntN(60) * time.Minute` at `:358` and `:384`; asserted against by `cmd/lctl/demo_coverage_test.go:534` | Hit once while running the full `make test-integration` for M39: *"Click history (M9) counted 34532 on the first run and 34540 after the switch"* — eight rows, the number of today's clicks whose random minute fell inside the minute the wall clock crossed between the first and third runs, which the logged runtimes put about ten seconds apart. Not reproducible on demand and it did not recur in four consecutive re-runs; the mechanism is nonetheless visible in the two lines above rather than inferred from the symptom, and it is independent of anything M39 changed — the seeder's click generation is untouched by this milestone. What M39 adds is two more service calls to each run, which lengthens a run by tens of milliseconds and therefore makes straddling a minute boundary marginally likelier | **Minor, and a flaky gate rather than a broken demo.** The demo it produces is correct either way; what fails is the assertion that two runs match, and it fails at random. The cost is that a red `make test-integration` has to be re-run to find out whether it meant anything, which is the property that makes a flake expensive. The fix is to truncate `now` — or to freeze it once per run and pass it to every generator — so the boundary is not crossed mid-comparison, rather than to relax the assertion. It belongs to [M33.5](phase-details/m33.5.md), whose claim is that this test is the thing stopping the demo rotting | No. Deliberately **not merged** with [F74](#closed) by M45's queue hygiene, 2026-08-04, for the reason F74 gives itself: its 252-click delta is two orders of magnitude past what the mechanism recorded here can produce, so folding it in would make this row's stated cause look confirmed. Fix this first — freezing `now` once per run is right either way — then check whether F74 closed with it. | **M45, 2026-08-04.** The mechanism this row records is real and was never the whole cause. `if at.After(now) { continue }` did drop a different handful of today's clicks between two runs — but the `continue` sat **inside** the sequence of PRNG draws for that click, so a discarded click consumed three fewer draws than a kept one and every link and every day generated after it came off a shifted stream. One click crossing the line therefore re-rolled the rest of the dataset, which is where [F74](#closed)'s unequal, unbounded, sometimes-negative deltas came from and why the failing assertion varied. Both halves are fixed in `cmd/lctl/demo_data.go`: every draw for a click is now taken before the discard, so a discard costs one row and nothing else; and the cutoff is `now.Truncate(time.Hour)` rather than the instant, so every run inside one hour generates the identical history. `demoClickRows` was split out of `demoClicks` so the property could be asserted on the generator — `cmd/lctl/demo_clicks_test.go`, two tests, each sabotage-verified against its own half. **Not F68**: `demoActor` (D61) pins the workspace before the reset runs, and `click_events` is truncated wholesale rather than by workspace, so nothing the counts measured was ever workspace-scoped. Reasoning, the alternatives refused and the residual accepted are [D99](decisions.md#2026-08-04--m45-the-demo-flake-was-the-random-stream-moving-not-the-minute) |
| F74 | M41 | `demoCoverage`'s idempotency assertion failed once with a **252-click** delta, which is two orders of magnitude larger than [F71](#closed)'s recorded mechanism can produce | `cmd/lctl/demo_coverage_test.go:570` — *"Click history (M9) counted 34042 on the first run and 33790 on the second"*, and the same pair again at `:604` for the third run. F71 attributes this assertion's flakiness to `cmd/lctl/demo_data.go:361` and `:387` (`if at.After(now) { continue }`), where two runs seconds apart drop a different handful of today's clicks | Observed once, on the first `GOFLAGS=-count=1 make test-integration` of M41's tree. **Not reproducible**: six subsequent runs of the same code passed — one full suite plus five runs of `-run TestDemoSeederShowsEveryFeature` — as did two intermediate runs with parts of M41's seeder disabled. The seeder's runtimes were logged at 3.308s and 3.356s, so the two runs were seconds apart, and F71's mechanism accounts for roughly the number of today's clicks whose random minute falls inside that window — F71 itself observed **eight**. 252 is a whole low-weight link's history, not a few seconds of one. So either F71's stated cause is incomplete or there is a second one; what is *not* plausible is that M41 caused it, because the seeder's click generation runs before `demoSeeder.run` and is untouched by this milestone, and disabling M41's seeding entirely was one of the runs that passed. **Observed a third time on 2026-08-04, during M45**, on a tree whose only change was this file: 34746 on the first run and 34898 on the second and third — a **152-click** delta, reported by both `demo_coverage_test.go:744` and `:778`, with logged seeder runtimes of 3.435s, 3.382s and 3.446s. Green on an immediate `GOFLAGS=-count=1` re-run. Two independent observations now sit two orders of magnitude above F71's mechanism, and the deltas are unequal (252, then 152), so whatever the second cause is, it is not a fixed quantity | **Minor, and test-only** — the demo it produces is correct either way; what fails is the claim that two runs match. Its cost is F71's cost: a red `make test-integration` that has to be re-run to find out whether it meant anything, in a loop that treats a failing gate as a stop condition. Filed separately from F71 rather than folded into it because the magnitude contradicts F71's stated mechanism, and a row that quietly absorbed a contradicting observation would make the recorded cause look confirmed. The fix F71 proposes — freeze `now` once per run and pass it to every generator — is still the right first move, and this row is the reason to check afterwards that it actually closed it | No. Deliberately **not merged** with [F71](#closed) by M45's queue hygiene, 2026-08-04, on this row's own reasoning: same test, same assertion, contradicting magnitudes, and a row that quietly absorbed a contradicting observation would make the recorded cause look confirmed. **Fifth observation, M45 group 3's acceptance gate, 2026-08-04:** failed once then passed on re-run, delta **127** on Click history (34950 vs 35077) and **85** on destination attribution — and the third assertion fired too, the one about the owner having switched workspace. Five recorded deltas now read 252, 152, 4, 67, 8 and 127, they are unequal, one was negative, and the measured base rate from a clean worktree at HEAD is roughly one failure in three or four runs. **That rate is now a release problem rather than only a finding**: `make release-check` needs a green integration run, and a gate that fails a quarter of the time blocks a tag at random and trains a reader to re-run rather than look. F71's recorded mechanism — a minute boundary — cannot produce unequal deltas of this size, so whatever the second cause is remains unidentified. **Sixth observation, M45 group 8's gate, 2026-08-04:** delta **21**, and this time *only* the owner-switched assertion fired, the other two passing in the same run. Recorded deltas are now 252, 152, 4, 67, 8, 127 and 21 — every one different, one negative, and the failing assertion varies between runs. Seven sightings across an afternoon in which six independent commits were gated. | **M45, 2026-08-04, by the same edit as [F71](#closed)** — and this row's refusal to merge is what found the cause. The magnitudes could not come from a minute boundary because the boundary was only the *trigger*; the amplifier was the PRNG stream moving under a discarded click, which re-rolls every link and day generated afterwards. That produces exactly what was recorded: deltas of any size, either sign, and a failing assertion that varies — the third limb fires alone most often because it sits furthest from the first run in wall-clock time. Measured after the fix at **0 failures in 60 consecutive runs** — `-run TestDemoSeederShowsEveryFeatureItClaimsTo`, `GOFLAGS=-count=1`, `-race`, each on a fresh database, 2026-08-04. Against the pre-fix rate of roughly one in three or four this row recorded, that outcome has probability under 1e-9, and it puts a 95% upper bound of 4.9% on the rate that survives. What remains is bounded and named in [D99](decisions.md#2026-08-04--m45-the-demo-flake-was-the-random-stream-moving-not-the-minute): two runs on either side of an hour boundary still differ, by that hour's clicks and by nothing else |
| F145 | PR #4 review | API-key rotation is impossible for any key holding a non-delegable scope, and the unit test for the escape route is inverted so it passes on the bug | `internal/auth/apikey_rotation.go:340-360`; `internal/auth/apikey_rotation_test.go:90-95` | Fable 5 review 2026-08-06, re-validated against 748346b. `narrowScopes` refuses on any **held** non-delegable scope before `requested` is first read at `:361`, so the route its own error message (`:352` "rotate into a set that omits it") and doc comment (`:336-338`) promise is unreachable. The scope map grew four times in Phase 2 (`apikey_rotation_test.go:78-82`), so keys this strands exist. The test's assertion is `if len(errs) == 0 { t.Fatalf }` under a comment saying the rotation succeeds — corrected to expect success, it fails today. | Major — the affected key can never rotate; revoke and re-mint loses the grace overlap rotation exists to provide. | Yes — owner directed fixes for the PR #4 review findings, 2026-08-06 | PR #4 fix series, 2026-08-06. `narrowScopes` resolves the successor set first and refuses only a non-delegable scope that survives into it — inherited or re-requested refuses, omitting goes through. The inverted assertion was corrected and run red before the fix; the re-request case is pinned; a new integration test plants a historical `webhooks.write` key (mint refuses the scope, so an `UPDATE` is the only faithful construction) and walks inherit→422, re-request→422, omit→201 with both keys verifying in grace. | F146 | PR #4 review | The mail scrubber indexes a lowercased copy and slices the original with those offsets, so a case-shifting rune before the recipient leaks the address it exists to redact, or panics the process | `internal/mail/mail.go:352-363` (`scrubAddress`), `:379-389` (`replaceFold`, asymmetric on both sides) | Fable 5 review 2026-08-06, re-validated. Go's `strings.ToLower` is not byte-length-preserving: 25 runes shrink (U+212A 3→1), two grow (U+023A/U+023E 2→3). Nine shrinking runes ahead of the address make the final iteration emit the **whole address unredacted** via `:358`; one growing rune makes `msg[i+len(target):]` index past the end — and `grep -rn "recover()"` over the tree returns nothing, `scrubAddress` runs in Drain's bare goroutine (`mail.go:255`), so the panic is a process crash. `scrub_test.go` is all-ASCII and blind to both. The review's U+0130 growth example is Python semantics, not Go's — noted so the regression test is built on the right rune. | Major — PII leak in logs or full-process crash, both relay-influenced. | Yes — owner directed fixes for the PR #4 review findings, 2026-08-06 | PR #4 fix series, 2026-08-06. Matching now walks the original string rune-wise (`indexFold`), so every offset is native to what gets sliced; `scrubAddress`'s inline loop was `replaceFold` verbatim and now calls it. Case-insensitivity is unchanged — the fold is the same `unicode.ToLower` the old code applied, kept deliberately over `EqualFold`'s wider SimpleFold chain. Three new tests ran red first: the nine-Kelvin leak (whole address emitted, marker torn), the U+023A slice-bounds panic, and the non-ASCII-local `replaceFold` panic. This corrects F109's fix, whose tests were all-ASCII. |
| F147 | PR #4 review | The automation watermark advances to the last capped subject's timestamp while the match queries resume strictly above it, so subjects tied on that timestamp past the 25-row cap are dropped from every future window | `internal/automation/automation.go:286`, `:258-261`; `internal/store/query/automation.sql:190-192`, `:213-216`, `:232-234` | Fable 5 review 2026-08-06, re-validated. A naive `>=` re-fires the boundary subject, which `automation.sql:147-152` and two tests forbid — the fix needs a cursor that matches the ORDER BY. | Major — silent permanent drops of rule work, no log, no retry. | Yes — owner directed fixes for the PR #4 review findings, 2026-08-06 | PR #4 fix series, 2026-08-06 (D106). The watermark is now the pair `(last_fired_at, last_fired_subject_id)` — migration 03600, all three matchers resume from it, the claim compare-and-sets both halves, a re-arm resets both, and a legacy NULL reads as uuid.Max so pre-migration boundaries stay spent. New integration test drives 28 links sharing one expiry through the 25-row cap and asserts every one fires exactly once; sabotage-verified by deadening the id tiebreak, which dropped the tie group's remainder exactly as the defect did. |
| F148 | PR #4 review | HostCache.Refresh has a lost-wakeup race that strands the pending flag with no reload running, and its error path drops the invalidation outright | `internal/redirect/hosts.go:188-205` | Fable 5 review 2026-08-06, re-validated. Terminal state: flag set, nobody running. Second limb the review missed: `:196` clears `pending` before `Reload` runs, and the error return at `:203` drops the armed invalidation with no race needed. The hourly `runHostReload` backstop is a no-op at `DOMAIN_VERIFY_INTERVAL=0` (`jobs.go:469`). The two atomics became one mutex-guarded state machine, so the worker's exit check and its retirement are a single critical section and the stranded state is unreachable; the error path re-arms the flag before retiring instead of dropping the invalidation. Reload keeps its exact semantics for boot and the hourly timer (F73's note stands), and already detaches and bounds itself, so the worker now carries the caller's context untouched. The package gained its first hosts_test.go: deterministic tests for both limbs via a stubbed loader, both run red against the atomics version before the fix. |

The only password write is `POST /account/password` (`internal/httpx/router.go:482`), behind `signedIn` | Found while validating the owner's question on [F140](#closed) — *is account recovery possible as long as a mailer is set up*. The answer is no, and the mailer is irrelevant: the mechanism does not exist to be enabled. Three things make it worse than an absent feature. **Plan.md does not list it** in *Not in Phase 2* or anywhere else, so it is not a deferred decision, it is a gap nobody recorded. **The product has every part needed to build it** — a token-hash pattern used twice (invitations, signup verification), an outbox, a mailer, and argon2 rehashing on `POST /account/password` — so its absence reads as an oversight rather than a choice. And it **compounds F140**: D98's instance principal is one account, that account's only credential is a password, and losing it now strands the instance with `psql` as the sole route back, which is precisely what F140 is about | **Moderate to major.** No data loss and no disclosure, and an operator with database access can always rewrite a hash — so it is an availability and product-completeness defect rather than a security one. It is the highest tier of that class: on an instance with real users, the first forgotten password is a support request the product cannot answer, and there is no workaround short of the operator editing the database on somebody's behalf, which is also the only route to re-appointing a lost instance principal | No, and deliberately still no. Reviewed 2026-08-05: the owner chose to **record the absence and schedule nothing**, so this row is now documented in the two places a reader looks — Plan.md's *Not in Phase 2* and README's *Not built yet* — and each says the concrete thing rather than the abstract one: a forgotten password cannot be recovered by the person who forgot it, a mailer does not change that because no mechanism exists to use one, and the operator's route is editing the database. Both name `lctl instance principal move` as covering the instance principal and nothing else. **The row stays Open**: it is documented, not resolved, and the first limb of this cell's own argument — that Plan.md does not list it — is the only one that has stopped being true. **Scheduled 2026-08-06 as [M51](phase-details/m51.md)**, Phase 3, and a hard prerequisite of [M53](phase-details/m53.md)'s second factor. Still open until M51 lands. | **[M51](phase-details/m51.md)**, 2026-08-07 — the milestone this row was scheduled as, and the whole of it. `password_resets` in migration `03900`, modelled on `pending_registrations`: a `token_hash bytea` unique index, an expiry, a `consumed_at`, and a partial index so the purge is an index range. **Two routes on each surface** — `GET/POST /forgot` and `GET/POST /reset/{token}` on the dashboard, `POST /api/v1/auth/forgot` and `POST /api/v1/auth/reset` on the API — in the public block under the same `guard(...)` as `POST /login`, so recovery and credential stuffing share one budget and the milestone adds no limiter of its own. The token is minted by `auth.NewOpaqueToken` and stored only as `auth.HashOpaqueToken`; a test greps the row for the plaintext it just issued. The new hash goes through `auth.WritePassword`, **extracted so there is one password-writing function rather than two call sites that agree** — `ChangePassword` calls it too. **Four refusals, one answer, each with its own test**: spent, lapsed, a status that is not `active`, and a null `password_hash` are all `404` and never `410`. **Enumeration**: the response is byte-identical whatever the address, `recovery.Requested` carries the normalized address and nothing else so there is no timestamp to keep identical (the residue F13's test found), and a dummy argon2 verification is spent before the lookup on every branch. An address that cannot be recovered gets a mail saying so, mirroring `account-exists` — one template for *no account* and *not recoverable*, per D144. **Blast radius**: every session revoked with none kept, every sibling token consumed, API keys deliberately untouched (D9, D87), audited as `password.reset` with the account as actor, an IP prefix and no organization. The purge joins the hourly `housekeeping` pass at `purgeBatch`, with no new job family. **One limb of the finding survives on purpose and is now a *Known limitations* row rather than this one**: the row said *whether or not a mailer is configured*, and a mailer is exactly what changes it — with `SMTP_HOST` unset there is still no recovery, and D143 is the decision that it **refuses out loud** rather than degrading, which is the one place this product breaks the mail-free pattern D1 established. That is a documented default, not the undocumented absence this row was filed about. D143–D145 carry the reasoning; Plan.md's *Not in Phase 2* row is amended rather than removed, so the Phase 2 deferral keeps its reason. |

Every attack on it failed — a non-superuser alters its own per-database settings with no grant, since these are USERSET; there is no `SET ROLE` escape hatch because `addons.go:417-418` logs the pool in **as** the role, which is why startup applies the rows; `pin` (`addons.go:1195-1197`) pins only `search_path` and `statement_timeout`, and replaying inside that transaction still reads 4GB. **Wider than first filed**: `IN DATABASE <any name>` is accepted including databases the role cannot connect to, so a one-database repair is also incomplete, and no `DROP ROLE` path exists anywhere in the product, so the rows outlive uninstall. **The test that guards this is structurally blind** — `test/integration/addon_storage_test.go:1432` sets the plain variant and its verifier `roleConfig` at `:1484` reads only `pg_roles` | **Medium.** Not a data-boundary breach — it reads no other tenant's rows. What holds instead is the post-condition, which refuses an add-on whose role carries a parked row and therefore needs no proof of ownership at all. The residue is stated in `m63.md`, `Plan.md`, `docs/deployment.md` and `docs/operations.md` rather than swept: an uninstalled add-on's settings stay on a role nothing drops, inert because only a **login** reads one and nothing logs in as that role, cleared by the add-on's next load or by an operator's `ALTER ROLE … RESET ALL`. Variation selectors are category `Mn`, so `unicode.IsGraphic` says yes and the default-deny fallback never reaches them. **The same failure shape as the hand-written list D242 replaced, one abstraction level up**: a property whose name begins with `Other_` is incomplete by construction. | `internal/addon/hostabi.go:470-495` — the property test at `:491`, the fallback at `:494`. Go's tables were checked against Unicode's rather than assumed — `Other_DI` has 3776 members of which 7 are graphic, `Variation_Selector` has 260, all graphic, and **none of them is in `Other_DI`**; `Cf` intersected with graphic is empty, so the fallback covers `Cf` and cannot reach the selectors. Works with a U+E0100 byte-per-selector scheme and a U+FE00–FE0F nibble scheme, so it does not depend on plane-14 support. No downstream filter exists: `hostabi.go:109` is the only call site, `internal/observability/logging.go:19-35`'s `ReplaceAttr` touches attribute keys and never `msg`, `strconv.Quote` preserves anything `IsPrint`, and `Mn` is printable. `maxLogMessage` bounds one line at roughly 682–1024 payload bytes and nothing bounds the call rate | **High.** It is the one boundary D240 exists for, it is reachable by every loaded module including one that declared nothing, and three shipped documents state the opposite. Reliability of the *hiding* is renderer-dependent — browser log UIs render a selector after a non-emoji base as nothing, a bare terminal with a `.notdef`-drawing font may show narrow boxes — which narrows the concealment and not the defect | Yes — owner approved 2026-08-20 on M64.9's triage; **reopens [M62](phase-details/m62.md)**. The remedy is **D270** and was answered in the same breath, because escaping all 260 would make `hostabi_test.go:638`'s assertion about `❤️` false and that is a choice rather than a patch | **M62**, reopened 2026-08-20 under D269, closed by **D283** — which supersedes D270's remedy rather than implementing it. The derivation is kept and is the half of D270 nothing disputed: `defaultIgnorable` computes `Other_DI ∪ Cf ∪ Variation_Selector − White_Space − FFF9..FFFB − 13430..13440 − PCM`, all seven terms, and `TestNoDefaultIgnorableCharacterReachesALogLine` counts 4174 members and 267 graphic against the residue property's 3776 and 7 and asserts that no member of the derived set reaches a line as itself. The derivation as first written for this row had six of the property's seven terms and so claimed 4190, and the count sat inside a floor where a wrong constant reads the same as a Unicode revision; the seventh term and equalities for every stated number are **D284**. **The carve-out is gone: every variation selector is deleted from the message rather than escaped, with no base set and no exemption** (`strippedRune`). `❤️` becomes `❤`, still a heart; `😀` is untouched. Two narrower remedies were built and rejected before this one, and neither shipped — both are on the unmerged branch `wip/m62-sanitizer`, and D276 and D277 are their numbers, spent there and withdrawn. Attempt 1 keyed the exemption on category `So` and a progress bar drawn from `U+2588` and `U+2591` carried the secret through byte-identical at log₂3 bits per symbol; attempt 2 keyed it on Unicode's registered emoji variation sequences and was defeated in turn by building the channel from registered bases whose selector a renderer ignores. Stripping became available once the threat was read as **legibility rather than confidentiality** — `log` is ungated, so a module may write a secret in plain text regardless, and what the boundary owes is only that what a reader sees is what is there. The lossiness that buys is proved harmless in `TestStrippingIsLossyAndForgesNothing`: the boundary is the escaping form composed after a deletion, so its image is a subset of the escaping form's and a module reaches strictly fewer log lines than before; the record boundary, the `addon` and `source` attributes, and the truncation mark's doubled backslash are each unaffected. The three reproduction schemes and the block-element bar are kept as tests, driven both at the boundary function and end to end through the `undeclared` fixture. Three documents that promised the wrong thing are corrected — and `docs/SECURITY.md` and `CHANGELOG.md` are corrected twice over, because the shipped sentence *cannot arrange bytes in an operator's log to be overlooked* and the parked attempt's replacement *cannot put invisible bytes in an operator's log* are both false, and what stands now claims only what default-ignorability supports. **The claim was narrowed once more before this row landed (D285), and the remainder is [F310](#open).** *Invisible* is not a Unicode property and this row's fix does not enforce one: eight combining marks Unicode annotates as not visibly rendered are outside `Default_Ignorable_Code_Point` and reach a line as themselves. What bounds them is a property the tree now asserts rather than one that merely held — an add-on may post to the log and may not read it, driven over every function in the ABI by `TestAnAddonPostsToTheLogAndCannotReadItBack`. And the neutralization was reaching one host function while three documents claimed it for the module: manifest validation embeds a refused value with `%q`, which escapes neither `Mn` nor `Lo`, so a hostile name, version or migration filename reached an operator's log and an instance's fatal message untouched. **The site list was the wrong shape and was wrong twice — the second one missed `store.MigrateAddon`'s success-path line, in a package that cannot import the neutralizer, and the per-request instantiation failure — so the escaping is applied by the logger the subsystem writes through and by a single neutralizing exit on `Route`, and there is no list (D286).** |

The host writes no cookie a module named. It packs an add-on's cookies into one cookie of its own per lifetime class — `linkctrl_addon_<name>` and `…_kept`, inside the namespace no manifest may declare a prefix in — and `Route` **empties the module's list as it packs**, so the writer in `internal/httpx` has no path to that list at all. **Not the fix the row's severity cell implies**, and the reason is in D287: every threshold shape is wrong here, because a browser cookie is persistent and the add-on also decides how many responses there are, so a cap per response is that cap times however many visits it can arrange. One threshold remains and is named as one: a jar holds 3 KiB, over which an add-on loses its own oldest values and the log names it. The two refuted limbs are not re-fixed — the 60 KB single cookie no browser stores, and `manifest.go`'s namespace message, which was never falsified. Sabotage, all restored by counter-edit: writing one cookie per entry fails four tests, dropping the jar-size refusal fails two, one jar for both lifetimes fails the lifetime test, dropping the log fails the full-jar test, and a writer that reaches for the module's list fails the writer test |
